### Visual: the congregational mosque, walked from the gate

```text
            N
            ^                         THE DIRECTIONAL PLAN
            |            (in India the qibla wall is on the WEST side,
            |             so the worshipper faces WEST towards Mecca)

   +=================================================================+
   |                        outer enclosure wall                     |
   |    [minar]                                             [minar]  |
   |       |                                                   |     |
   |   +---+---------------------------------------------------+--+  |
   |   |                                                          |  |
   |   |                       S A H N                            |  |
   |   |                (open courtyard, often with               |  |
   |   |                 an ablution tank at centre)              |  |
   |   |                          O  <- tank                      |  |
   |   |                                                          |  |
   |   +==========================================================+  |
   |   |   PISHTAQ  (tall rectangular frame around the arch)      |  |
   |   |      +--------------------------------------+            |  |
   |   |      |   IWAN  (vaulted hall, open on one   |            |  |
   |   |      |          side, set in the frame)     |            |  |
   |   |      +--------------------------------------+            |  |
   |   |                                                          |  |
   |   |          L I W A N  /  prayer hall (covered)             |  |
   |   |   . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .    |  |
   |   |   . rows of worshippers stand parallel to this wall .    |  |
   |   |   . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .    |  |
   |   |                         [ ] <- MIHRAB (niche)            |  |
   |   +==========================================================+  |
   |                    Q I B L A   W A L L                          |
   +=================================================================+
                    (the wall oriented towards Mecca)

   ENTRY  ->  sahn  ->  ablution  ->  liwan  ->  rows facing the
                                                 qibla wall / mihrab
```

Read the plan as a sequence of decisions, not a set of labels. One wall is privileged by the direction of prayer; the covered hall is therefore placed against that wall; rows of worshippers form parallel to it, which means the hall wants to be **wide rather than deep** - and a wide, column-free hall is precisely what the arch and the dome make buildable. The courtyard exists because congregational prayer overflows. The great framed arch exists because a wide hall needs a legible centre. The minars exist to mark the building from a distance. Every element is a consequence.

### 1. The mosque, part by part

| Term | Precise meaning | What it generates in the plan |
|---|---|---|
| **Qibla** | The direction of prayer, towards Mecca; in India this lies to the west | Privileges one wall and fixes the building's orientation |
| **Qibla wall** | The wall of the mosque aligned to that direction | The hall is placed against it; the whole plan is measured from it |
| **Mihrab** | A niche, usually semicircular and recessed, set in the qibla wall | Marks the direction visually and acoustically; the focal point of ornament |
| **Sahn** | The open courtyard | Accommodates overflow congregation; usually holds the ablution tank |
| **Liwan** | The covered prayer hall, often a vaulted or many-columned space | The wide, shallow hall that the arcuate system makes possible |
| **Iwan** | A vaulted hall or rectangular space walled on three sides with one side entirely open | Used as the monumental entrance to a hall or courtyard |
| **Pishtaq** | A formal rectangular frame enclosing an arched opening, usually taller than the arch inside it | Gives verticality and emphasis to the entrance; the facade's principal device |
| **Minar** | A tall tower attached to or standing near a mosque or mausoleum | The distant marker; recorded as introduced by the Islamic rulers around mosques and mausoleums |

**Beyond the standard account.** Of these, only **minar**, **jaali** and **charbagh** are defined in the standard material for this topic. **Qibla, qibla wall, mihrab, sahn, liwan, iwan and pishtaq** are not. The definitions given above are ordinary architectural terminology rather than contested claims, and none of them is used below to support a disputed attribution.

**Where the vocabulary pays.** The Buland Darwaza is exactly a pishtaq problem: an enormous rectangular frame containing a recessed arched half-dome, forming the grand entrance of the Jama Masjid at Fatehpur Sikri. A candidate who names the device is describing; a candidate who merely calls it "a huge gateway" is gesturing. Likewise, Fatehpur Sikri's **Diwan-i-Khas** is described in the standard account in terms that only this vocabulary handles: raised on a square plan, **outwardly giving the appearance of a double-storeyed structure**, but internally **a single vaulted chamber open from floor to roof, with an opening on each of the four sides**. That is a deliberate disjunction between exterior reading and interior reality - the same trick as a double dome, executed on a whole building.

### 2. The tomb, and why it is a different kind of plan

A mosque is directional. A tomb is centralised: the point of interest is in the middle, so the building tends to become symmetrical on all four sides, often square or octagonal in plan, often with an entrance on each face, often raised on a plinth or terrace, and usually crowned by a dome over the central chamber.

Parts worth naming:

- the **chamber**, the domed space at the centre;
- the **cenotaph**, the visible memorial above, which is not the burial;
- the **crypt or grave chamber**, usually below, containing the actual burial;
- the **plinth or terrace**, raising the structure and setting it apart from the garden or court;
- the **chhatri**, the small domed kiosk on pillars used on roofs and corners, which is where the tomb type picks up an indigenous element.

The standard account supplies the octagonal Lodi tomb type directly: octagonal, **roughly 15 m in diameter**, **hard and bare, without lavish decorations**, and **supported by a sloping verandah**. It supplies Humayun's tomb as **commissioned by Bega Begum in 1558**, after Humayun's death, and records its inscription as a **UNESCO World Heritage Site in 1993**. It supplies Sher Shah Suri's tomb at his birthplace **Sasaram**, made of **red sandstone** and **situated inside a lake**.

Read those three together and the typological progression is visible without any interpretation being added: a bare octagon in a garden; a red sandstone mass on a terrace in the middle of water; a monumental garden tomb at the centre of a formal enclosure.

### 3. The garden, the water and the charbagh

The standard account treats water as a designed element with a stated purpose list. **An important feature of the architecture during this period was the use of water in the premises of the constructions in the form of courtyard pools, fountains and small drains.** Water was used primarily for three purposes: **religious purpose**, **to cool the premises**, and **decorative purpose**. And the rulers **introduced the Charbagh style of gardening, in which a square block was divided into four adjacent identical gardens**.

That definition is compact and examinable, and it is often put more briefly still as **a square garden divided into four identical quadrants**.

What the plan does, stated as an argument:

- **It organises.** Two intersecting axes, usually channels, divide the square into four. The crossing point is the privileged position, and it is where a pavilion, a tank or - in a garden tomb - the mausoleum itself is placed.
- **It distributes water.** The channels are not decorative lines; they are the irrigation and cooling system, fed from tanks and moving by gravity, with fountains and small drains as the standard account records.
- **It signifies.** A quadripartite garden with water running through it is a legible reference to a paradise garden. But the signification only works because the geometry is strict, which is why the garden is as much an engineering artefact as a symbolic one.
- **It sets the building.** A garden tomb is the combination of the centralised tomb type with the charbagh: the building occupies the crossing of the axes, so that every approach is axial and every view is symmetrical.

The standard account records **Bagh-e Babur in Kabul, built in 1504** after Babur's conquest of Kabul in the same year, as **one of the earliest surviving Mughal gardens in the world**; **Charbagh-style gardens inside Agra Fort**; **Shalimar Bagh in Kashmir** designed under Jahangir and **Shalimar Bagh in Lahore** under Shah Jahan; and **Charbagh-style gardens** as one of the Taj Mahal's listed features.

### 4. The fort, the palace and the gate, kept in proportion

This topic owns forts and palaces as architecture, and the standard account gives enough for a competent treatment without straying into military history.

- **Agra Fort**: one of the first constructions begun in Akbar's reign, **built between 1565 and 1573**, though **most of the structures present inside the fort were constructed during the reign of Shah Jahan** - the Moti Masjid, the Diwan-i-Aam and the Diwan-i-Khas among them, with the Jahangiri Mahal and the Sheesh Mahal also named. Its gardens are **Charbagh style**.
- **Allahabad Fort**: built by Akbar in **1583**, near the **confluence of the Yamuna with the Ganges**; the **Allahabad Pillar of Ashoka** is presently located inside its premises.
- **Rohtas Fort**, built by **Sher Shah**, is recorded as being **in Pakistan**.
- **Siri Fort** is recorded among the Khilji works.
- **Tughlaqabad, Jahanpanah and Ferozabad** are recorded as Tughlaq **cities**, which is a different category again - not single buildings but planned urban foundations.
- **Shahjahanabad** is recorded as a city built by Shah Jahan.

The examinable point is that this tradition builds at four different scales - the single structure, the complex, the fort, and the city - and that the largest of these is a planning act. Lesson 19 develops that.

### Comparative note: three plan types, one vocabulary

| Dimension | Mosque | Tomb | Garden |
|---|---|---|---|
| Organising principle | Direction (qibla) | Centre (the chamber) | Geometry (the two axes) |
| Consequence for form | One privileged wall; wide shallow hall | Four-sided symmetry; dome over the middle | Four equal quadrants; crossing point privileged |
| Signature element | Mihrab in the qibla wall | Cenotaph above, burial below | Channels, tanks, fountains and drains |
| Where the two others appear | Courtyard may be laid out geometrically | Often set at the crossing of a charbagh | Frequently contains a pavilion or a tomb |

The three types are not alternatives; they combine. A garden tomb is types two and three fused. A mosque inside a fort is type one embedded in a fourth scale. Recognising the combination is usually the quickest route into a description.

### Criticism and reply: is the charbagh really a "paradise garden", or is that a later reading?

**The objection.** Calling every quadripartite garden a paradise garden risks importing a fixed symbolic meaning into what may simply be an efficient irrigation layout. Four quarters fed by gravity channels is a sensible way to water a square plot on any continent, and the symbolic gloss may be a modern art-historical overlay.

**The reply.** Two things keep the symbolic reading defensible without overclaiming. First, the source evidence in this topic is explicit that water in these premises served a **religious purpose** alongside cooling and decoration - so a non-utilitarian dimension is recorded, not inferred. Second, the symbolic reading is strongest exactly where the geometry is most insisted upon and least necessary for irrigation: a strictly equal four-part division, centred on a tomb, with the building raised on a terrace at the crossing, is doing something beyond watering plants.

**The balanced verdict.** Treat the charbagh as simultaneously hydraulic and symbolic, and say so. In an answer, the sentence to write is: "The charbagh is an irrigation geometry that was made to carry a meaning, and the evidence for the meaning is the insistence on exact quadripartition where mere watering would not have required it."

### Examination use

A **regional-variation demand** needs this vocabulary because provincial schools are distinguished precisely by what they do to these elements - Jaunpur's screen across the prayer-hall front, Malwa's and Jaunpur's refusal of minars, Bengal's roof over the same liwan. A **synthesis demand** needs it because the plan requirements are the clearest "imported" half of the synthesis: qibla orientation, the mihrab, the minar and the charbagh are programme, not technique. A **Mughal-development demand** needs the garden tomb, because that is the type the dynasty made its own. And an **ideology demand** needs the pishtaq and the gateway, because that is where scale becomes statement.

### Revision notes

1. Qibla = the direction of prayer towards Mecca; in India it lies to the west. The qibla wall is aligned to it.
2. Mihrab = the niche in the qibla wall marking the direction; the focal point of the prayer hall's ornament.
3. Sahn = the open courtyard, usually with an ablution tank. Liwan = the covered prayer hall against the qibla wall.
4. Iwan = a vaulted space walled on three sides with one side fully open. Pishtaq = the tall rectangular frame enclosing an arched opening.
5. Minar = a tall tower near a mosque or mausoleum; recorded as introduced by the Islamic rulers around mosques and mausoleums; absent in trabeate practice.
6. Of these, only minar, jaali and charbagh are defined in the standard material; the rest are ordinary architectural terminology supplied here.
7. The prayer hall is wide and shallow because rows form parallel to the qibla wall - which is why a column-free wide span is worth having.
8. Tomb parts: chamber, cenotaph above, burial crypt below, plinth or terrace, chhatri kiosks.
9. Lodi tomb type: octagonal, about 15 m diameter, hard and bare, sloping verandah.
10. Humayun's tomb: commissioned by Bega Begum in 1558 after Humayun's death; UNESCO World Heritage Site 1993.
11. Sher Shah Suri's tomb: at Sasaram, his birthplace; red sandstone; situated inside a lake.
12. Water in these premises: courtyard pools, fountains and small drains, for religious, cooling and decorative purposes.
13. Charbagh = a square block divided into four adjacent identical gardens; the crossing of the axes is the privileged position.
14. Named gardens on record: Bagh-e Babur, Kabul, 1504, one of the earliest surviving Mughal gardens; Charbagh gardens inside Agra Fort; Shalimar Bagh, Kashmir, under Jahangir; Shalimar Bagh, Lahore, under Shah Jahan; Charbagh gardens at the Taj Mahal.
15. Four scales of building: the structure, the complex, the fort, the planned city - Tughlaqabad, Jahanpanah, Ferozabad, Shahjahanabad.
16. Agra Fort: begun under Akbar, built between 1565 and 1573; most interior structures are Shah Jahan's. Allahabad Fort: built by Akbar in 1583 at the Yamuna-Ganges confluence; holds the Allahabad Ashokan pillar.
17. Diwan-i-Khas at Fatehpur Sikri: square plan, outwardly appearing double-storeyed, internally a single vaulted chamber open from floor to roof with an opening on each of the four sides.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the requirement to face Mecca, rather than any stylistic preference, accounts for the characteristic shape of a congregational mosque's prayer hall - and what that has to do with the arch.

**Model answer:** Prayer is performed facing the qibla, so one wall of the mosque is privileged and the congregation forms in long rows parallel to it. The useful dimension of the hall is therefore its width along that wall, not its depth away from it, because a worshipper in the tenth row gains nothing over one in the second. This generates a hall that is wide and shallow and that ideally has as few internal obstructions as possible, since every column interrupts a row. A trabeate hall cannot satisfy that requirement well: short lintel spans force closely spaced supports, producing exactly the forest of columns that breaks the rows. The arcuate system removes the constraint, because arches, vaults and domes span far further with stone working in compression. The plan requirement and the structural technology are therefore not two separate imports that happened to arrive together; the programme creates the demand that the technology answers.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the mosque plan as an aesthetic template that was carried to India and copied. It is a functional specification, and that is why it produced genuinely different buildings in Bengal, Jaunpur, Malwa, the Deccan, Gujarat and Kashmir: the specification stayed constant while material, climate, craft tradition and politics varied, and the variation is where the marks are.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Examine the charbagh as both an engineering layout and a symbolic statement, with examples. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

The charbagh is a square garden divided by intersecting axes into four identical quadrants, and it functions on both registers at once.

As engineering it is a gravity-fed water system. The source evidence records courtyard pools, fountains and small drains as designed elements, and identifies cooling among the three purposes water served; the channels that divide the quarters are the distribution network, and the crossing is its hydraulic centre.

As statement it is a legible geometry of order. The insistence on exact quadripartition exceeds what irrigation requires, and the same evidence records a religious purpose alongside cooling and decoration. Placing a mausoleum at the crossing, as at Humayun's tomb, converts the layout into a claim about the dead.

Bagh-e Babur of 1504, the Charbagh gardens within Agra Fort and those of the Taj Mahal show the type carried across three generations, always as both system and sign.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the exact definition of quadripartition 2 + the hydraulic reading with the pools-fountains-drains evidence 3 + the symbolic reading grounded in the recorded religious purpose 2 + the argument that exactness beyond irrigation need is what licenses the symbolic claim 2 + at least two named gardens 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 5:** The plan is settled, but almost nothing about an Indo-Islamic building's *appearance* has been explained yet. What covers the wall, and why? The next lesson takes the surface - the writing, the vegetal ornament, the pierced screen, the inlay - and treats each as a device with a job, including one device whose standard textbook description is technically wrong.

## Lesson 5 - The surface: calligraphy, arabesque, jali, inlay, and the elements that came the other way

Progress: 5/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the ornament grammar, the figural-imagery rule and its limits, optical scaling against foreshortening, and chhatri, bracket, batter and the bangaldar roof as elements moving into the tradition

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the features list, the arabesque and jaali passages, and the pietra-dura entry
CA search: "Not required - ornament and vocabulary lesson"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: what the wall is doing, layer by layer

```text
   OUTSIDE                                                     INSIDE
      |                                                           |
   +--v-----------------------------------------------------------v--+
   |  SURFACE SYSTEM 4  -  INLAY / PIETRA DURA                      |
   |     cut coloured and semi-precious stones set into marble       |
   |     (parchin kari) - the latest and most expensive layer        |
   +-----------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  SURFACE SYSTEM 3  -  CALLIGRAPHY                              |
   |     writing used as the principal decorative system, replacing  |
   |     figural sculpture; enlarged high on a facade so that it     |
   |     READS EVENLY from the ground (optical scaling)              |
   +-----------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  SURFACE SYSTEM 2  -  ARABESQUE                                |
   |     continuous stem, splitting REGULARLY into counterpoised     |
   |     leafy secondary stems, which split again or return and      |
   |     merge with the main stem                                    |
   +-----------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  SURFACE SYSTEM 1  -  GEOMETRY                                 |
   |     the underlying setting-out: symmetry, repetition, grids     |
   +-----------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  THE WALL ITSELF  -  and where it is PERFORATED:                |
   |     J A L I  -  pierced screen; light and air pass, the eye     |
   |     is filtered; "signifies the importance of light"            |
   +-----------------------------------------------------------------+

   ELEMENTS MOVING THE OTHER WAY, INTO THE SAME BUILDINGS:
     chhatri (domed kiosk on pillars) | bracket (corbelled support)
     batter (deliberately sloping wall) | bangaldar roof (curved,
     from the Bengal hut, later borrowed by Mughal architects)
     | motifs: lotus, bell, swastika
```

Read the diagram vertically and the argument is visible. The surface is not "decoration"; it is a stack of systems, each with a job. And the bottom band of the diagram is the part candidates forget: traffic on this wall runs in both directions.

### 1. The image policy, stated exactly and then qualified

The standard account records that **Indo-Islamic architecture avoided the use of human and animal figures**, and that whereas **the previous structures used sculptures as a means of decoration, the Indo-Islamic architecture used calligraphy as a means of decoration**.

That is the rule, and it explains a great deal: if you cannot carve a figure, the decorative load has to be carried by something else, and writing, vegetal ornament and geometry are what carry it. This is not a deprivation; it is a redirection of enormous craft skill into three highly developed systems.

But the rule needs a qualification: **avoidance of figural imagery is general for mosque decoration, and is not a universal rule for every Indo-Islamic or secular building.** Palace, garden and secular contexts behave differently, and painting under the same patrons - Topic 07's material - is emphatically figural. An answer that states the rule without the qualification is stating a half-truth; an answer that states both is showing judgement.

### 2. Arabesque, defined precisely because the definition is examinable

The standard account's definition is unusually exact and should be learned close to its wording. **Arabesque meant the use of geometrical vegetal ornamentation and was characterised by a continuous stem which split regularly, producing a series of counterpoised, leafy, secondary stems. The secondary stems, in turn, split again or returned to merge with the main stem, creating a decorative pattern.**

Three features are being specified. The ornament is **vegetal in reference but geometrical in construction**. The splitting is **regular**, not naturalistic. And the system is **closed and self-returning** - secondary stems either split again or come back to the main stem, so the pattern can extend indefinitely without ever terminating in a fixed image. That last property is the point: arabesque is a system for covering any area of any shape, which is exactly what a building needs.

The standard account also records that the architecture of the period **heavily used the principles of geometry - from decorative pattern to imbibing a sense of symmetry**. Geometry is thus both the ornament's construction method and the building's compositional method.

### 3. Jali, and the claim that goes with it

**Jaali** is the perforated stone or lattice screen. The standard account states that the buildings had **intricate jaali works, which signifies the importance of light in Islamic religion** - a **perforated stone or lattice screen, signifying the importance of light in Islamic religious space**.

What a jali physically does is worth spelling out, because it is a genuinely clever piece of environmental design:

- it **admits light while breaking glare**, turning a harsh opening into a diffused one;
- it **admits air**, so a chamber can be ventilated without being exposed;
- it **allows vision outward while restricting vision inward**, which is why it appears in screened viewing positions;
- it **reduces the apparent mass** of a wall, so a heavy masonry enclosure can be made to read as light.

The standard account attaches jali to two specific monuments. The **tomb of Sheikh Salim Chishti** at Fatehpur Sikri, constructed entirely in white marble, **features exquisite jaali screens with complex geometric patterns, black marble inscriptions, and painted floral motifs**. And at the **Taj Mahal**, the **jaali work is lace-like and extremely fine**, alongside marble carvings **in low relief**.

### 4. Calligraphy, and the correction that earns marks

The standard account records a device it calls **foreshortening**: **a unique feature was the use of foreshortening technique in the buildings, such that the inscriptions appear to be closer than they really are.**

That terminology is loose, and the correction is examinable. The device is **optical scaling in calligraphy**: letters placed high on a facade may be **enlarged** so that the inscription **appears visually even from ground level**. It is **not the same as pictorial foreshortening**.

The distinction is real and worth holding.

- **Pictorial foreshortening** is a drawing technique: representing an object as receding, by shortening it along the line of sight.
- **Optical scaling** is a compensation technique: progressively enlarging letters or panels with height so that perspective diminution is cancelled and the whole inscription reads at a uniform apparent size from the viewer's position on the ground.

An answer that writes "foreshortening" is repeating a textbook formula. An answer that writes "letters were graded in size with height so that the inscription reads evenly from the ground" is describing what the mason actually did. The second is worth more, and it also makes the Taj Mahal's listed feature - which the standard account groups with calligraphy and pietra dura - intelligible instead of mysterious.

### 5. Pietra dura, named in two languages

**Pietra dura**, also called **parchin kari**, is the inlay of cut coloured stones, often semi-precious, into a marble or other stone ground. The standard account records the technique as used **for inlay of precious coloured stones and gems into the walls for decoration**, and attaches its finest examples to the **tomb of Itmad-ud-Daulah**, which it records as having **some of the finest pietra-dura works**, and to the **Taj Mahal**, where it is listed among the monument's features.

Two analytical points follow.

- **It is a marble technique.** Inlay of this precision requires a fine, workable, pale ground. The arrival of pietra dura at scale is therefore tied to the arrival of white marble as the chief building material, which is Lesson 14's material argument.
- **It is a workshop technique.** Cutting, fitting and polishing thousands of small shaped stones is a division-of-labour problem, not a single artist's achievement. It belongs with the collective-workshop argument that Lesson 15 uses against single-genius accounts of the Taj.

### 6. The traffic in the other direction

This is the part of the lesson that carries the synthesis argument, and it is the part most often omitted.

- **Chhatri.** The small domed kiosk carried on pillars, used on roofs, parapets and corners. It is an indigenous element that becomes a standard component of the tradition, and the standard account records **multiple chhatris or kiosks on the top of the construction** as a defining feature of the Sikh idiom.
- **Bracket.** The corbelled support projecting from a wall or pillar to carry a beam, balcony or eave. It is trabeate in principle and indigenous in craft, and it is what makes the jharokha - the hanging balcony of Lesson 17 - structurally possible.
- **Batter.** The deliberate inward slope of a wall from base to top, giving stability. The standard account records that the Tughlaqs **introduced a style of construction known as batter, characterised by sloping walls to give more strength to the building**, and that the Malwa school used **the batter system as introduced by the Tughlaqs** to make its buildings strong. Batter is a structural answer to the outward thrust and the mass problem of Lesson 2.
- **The bangaldar roof.** The curved roof profile derived from the thatched bamboo hut of Bengal, translated into masonry. The standard account records that Bengal's mosques **continued the use of sloping Bangla roofs, which were previously used for temples**, and that the Bengal sloping roof was **later borrowed by Mughal architects**. The related family terms are **bangaldar** for the curved form, **do-chala** for a two-sided sloping roof and **chau-chala** for a four-sided one, and the Mughal adoption of these Bengal roof forms is significant in later north Indian building.
- **Motifs.** The fuller historical treatment of the period records that Sultanate and Mughal builders **reused local motifs such as the lotus, the bell and the swastika**.

The cumulative force of the list is the whole anti-essentialist case, made from objects rather than from sentiment. A tradition that takes in the hut roof of Bengal, the corbelled bracket of the Indian mason, the domed kiosk of Indian roofscapes and the lotus, bell and swastika as ornament is not an imported template applied to a passive landscape.

### Comparative note: four ornament systems and what each is for

| System | Construction | What it can do | Where it fails |
|---|---|---|---|
| Geometry | Ruled setting-out, symmetry, repetition | Cover any surface; organise a whole facade | Carries no content of its own |
| Arabesque | Continuous stem splitting regularly into counterpoised leafy secondaries | Fill irregular fields indefinitely without terminating | Cannot state anything specific |
| Calligraphy | Writing, graded in size with height so it reads evenly | Carry explicit content - text, name, date, claim | Requires literacy in the viewer to carry full content |
| Inlay | Cut coloured and semi-precious stones set into a marble ground | Introduce colour and precision at close range | Expensive; needs a fine pale ground; reads poorly at distance |

The table is also an argument: these four are not interchangeable. An answer that says "the buildings were richly decorated" has not noticed that the decoration is a system of four specialised tools deployed at four different viewing distances.

### Examination use

This lesson is the vocabulary bank that upgrades every other answer in the topic. Three specific pay-offs. First, the **optical scaling correction** is a small, precise, checkable point that separates a candidate who has thought about the building from one who has copied a phrase. Second, the **figural-imagery rule plus its qualification** is a ready-made demonstration of judgement in any synthesis answer. Third, the **traffic-in-the-other-direction list** - chhatri, bracket, batter, bangaldar roof, lotus, bell and swastika - is the single most efficient block of evidence available for the anti-essentialism argument, because it is concrete, short and hard to dispute.

### Revision notes

1. Recorded rule: Indo-Islamic architecture avoided human and animal figures; calligraphy replaced sculpture as the means of decoration.
2. Qualification: figural avoidance is general for mosque decoration, not a universal rule for every Indo-Islamic or secular building.
3. Arabesque: geometrical vegetal ornamentation; a continuous stem splitting regularly into counterpoised leafy secondary stems, which split again or return to merge with the main stem.
4. The architecture heavily used the principles of geometry, from decorative pattern to a sense of symmetry.
5. Jaali: perforated stone or lattice screen; the buildings had intricate jaali work, which signifies the importance of light in Islamic religion.
6. Jali does four things: diffuses light, admits air, permits outward vision while restricting inward vision, and lightens the apparent mass of a wall.
7. Named jali examples: the white-marble tomb of Sheikh Salim Chishti at Fatehpur Sikri, with complex geometric patterns, black marble inscriptions and painted floral motifs; and the lace-like, extremely fine jaali of the Taj Mahal, whose marble carvings are in low relief.
8. The standard account calls the calligraphic device "foreshortening"; the accurate term is optical scaling - letters high on a facade are enlarged so the inscription appears visually even from the ground, which is not pictorial foreshortening.
9. Pietra dura, also parchin kari: inlay of cut coloured and often semi-precious stones into marble. Finest works recorded at Itmad-ud-Daulah's tomb; listed among the Taj Mahal's features.
10. Pietra dura is tied to the marble era and to workshop organisation, which is why it supports the collective-authorship argument.
11. Traffic the other way: chhatri (domed kiosk, a defining Sikh feature), bracket (corbelled support enabling the jharokha), batter (sloping walls introduced by the Tughlaqs and reused in Malwa), bangaldar roof (Bengal's curved hut roof, later borrowed by Mughal architects), and the lotus, bell and swastika motifs.
12. Roof family terms: bangaldar for the curved form, do-chala for two-sided and chau-chala for four-sided sloping roofs.
13. Four ornament systems at four viewing distances: geometry organises, arabesque fills, calligraphy states, inlay refines at close range.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes that Indo-Islamic buildings used "foreshortening so that inscriptions appear closer than they are." What is technically wrong with this, and what should replace it?

**Model answer:** Foreshortening is a representational technique from drawing and painting: an object is depicted as compressed along the line of sight to suggest recession. Nothing of that kind is happening on a masonry facade, where the inscription is a real object at a real distance and no illusion of depth is being constructed. What the masons actually did was compensate for perspective diminution: letters and panels placed high on a wall were cut progressively larger, so that the whole inscription subtends a roughly uniform apparent size to a viewer standing at ground level and reads evenly from bottom to top. The correct description is therefore optical scaling in calligraphy, which is a different operation from pictorial foreshortening. The replacement sentence is: "Inscriptions were graded in size with height so that they read evenly from the ground."

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that reproducing a textbook's technical vocabulary is always safe. Terms migrate and get misapplied in study material; where a more accurate term exists, it is the one that demonstrates understanding. The same discipline applies to two other habits in this topic - calling every quadripartite garden a "Mughal garden" regardless of period, and calling every pierced screen a "jharokha", which is a balcony and not a screen.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Ornament in Indo-Islamic architecture is a system of specialised tools, not a general decorative impulse." Discuss. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

The claim holds, because each system does work the others cannot.

Geometry sets out the field, imposing symmetry and repetition across a whole facade, but carries no content. Arabesque fills irregular areas indefinitely through a continuous stem splitting regularly into counterpoised leafy secondaries that split again or return to the main stem, but again states nothing specific. Calligraphy carries explicit content - name, text, claim - and is graded in size with height so that it reads evenly from the ground. Inlay of cut coloured stones into marble, as at Itmad-ud-Daulah's tomb, adds colour and precision at close range, where the other three lose effect.

The systems are therefore matched to viewing distances and to functions, and they expanded because figural decoration was generally avoided in mosque contexts.

Calling this a decorative impulse misses the specialisation that makes it legible.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = all four systems named with a distinct function each 4 + the viewing-distance argument that explains their coexistence 2 + the exact arabesque definition 2 + one named monument carrying inlay 1 + the qualification that figural avoidance is a mosque-context rule 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 6:** Structure, plan and surface are now available as a complete reading kit. Time to use them on the first buildings the kit was invented for. The earliest Delhi work is also the most ethically demanding material in the topic, because it involves reuse of earlier structures and materials - and because the evidence for that reuse is specific, limited, and constantly over-generalised.
## Lesson 6 - The first Delhi buildings: adaptation, reuse and the discipline the evidence demands

### Visual: one tower, three reigns, and two accounts of the same fact

```text

  storey 5   ----------------------------  FIROZ SHAH TUGHLAQ
                                           one account: "the fifth storey
                                           was built by Firoz Shah Tughlaq"
                                           another account: he "repaired /

                                           --> BOTH POSITIONS RECORDED
  storey 4   ----------------------------
  storey 3   ----------------------------  ILTUTMISH
  storey 2   ----------------------------  one account: "the next three
                                           storeys were completed by
                                           Iltutmish"
                                           another account: "substantially
                                           completed by Iltutmish"
  storey 1   ----------------------------  QUTB-UD-DIN AIBAK
  ==========================================  "built only the ground floor"
             ground

  ONE MONUMENT  =  THREE PATRONS  =  MORE THAN A CENTURY
  --> never write "Qutb-ud-Din Aibak built the Qutb Minar"

  THE QUWWAT-UL-ISLAM REUSE CLAIM - TWO RECORDS

  textbook account         |  the ASI record of the complex
  -------------------------|------------------------------------------
  the mosque "was          |  the mosque INSCRIPTION records reuse of
  converted from a         |  MATERIAL from 27 demolished Hindu and
  Jain temple"             |  Jaina temples
  -------------------------|------------------------------------------
  a CONVERSION claim       |  a MATERIAL-REUSE claim, with a stated
  about one building       |  number and a stated documentary basis
  -------------------------|------------------------------------------
  --> these are different claims. Use the documented one. Record both.
  --> and do NOT generalise either into "temples were destroyed to
      build mosques across the period"
```

The diagram is the lesson. Two of the most quoted facts in this topic each exist in two versions, and the difference between the versions is precisely the difference between a careless answer and a careful one.

### 1. Why this lesson is placed here, and what it is not

The first Delhi buildings are where the reading kit of Lessons 2 to 5 was invented, so they come first among the monuments. They are also where the topic's only genuinely sensitive material sits. This lesson handles it the way the evidence requires: state exactly what is recorded, state exactly what is not, and refuse both the sanitising and the sensationalising version.

This is not a lesson about religious conflict, which is not this topic's property. It is a lesson about **spolia** - the direct architectural reuse of earlier structures or materials - as an evidentiary category, and about how a specific, documented instance is used in an answer without becoming a general claim it cannot support.

### 2. The Slave or Mameluke phase, and its building method

The standard account records the **Slave dynasty, also called the Ilbari dynasty, in power from 1206 to 1290 AD**, with the style of the period known as the **Mameluke style of architecture**. Its crucial sentence about method is this: during this period **most of the constructions were remodellings of the existing Hindu structures**, and the rulers **also started the erection of a number of monuments**.

Two things are being said. Remodelling of existing structures was the dominant early mode; and new construction began alongside it. An answer that reports only the first is describing a period that also raised the Qutb Minar from the ground; an answer that reports only the second is describing a period whose method was in fact largely adaptive. Both halves belong.

Why adaptation dominated early is not mysterious, and it is worth stating as a practical argument rather than an ideological one. A new regime needs congregational space immediately; existing masonry, existing quarries, existing masons and existing dressed stone are the fastest route to it; and the masons available worked by trabeate and corbelled methods, which is why the first great screen at Delhi is corbelled rather than arcuate, as Lesson 2 established.

### 3. The Qutb Minar, read as a record rather than as a monument

The standard account: the Qutb Minar is a **five-storey structure initiated by Qutb-ud-Din Aibak, who built only the ground floor**; **the next three storeys were completed by Iltutmish**; and **the fifth storey was built by Firoz Shah Tughlaq**.

A second formulation of the third stage is in circulation: the Minar was **begun by Qutb-ud-Din Aibak and substantially completed by Iltutmish**, with **Firoz Shah Tughlaq later repairing or rebuilding damaged upper work**.

**A discrepancy worth stating rather than resolving.** The two accounts agree on Aibak's ground floor and on Iltutmish's decisive contribution. They differ on whether Firoz Shah Tughlaq **built** the fifth storey as new work or **repaired and rebuilt** damaged upper portions. Both are recorded here; where a single phrase is needed, use the neutral "later upper-storey work under Firoz Shah Tughlaq" rather than pretending the conflict is absent.

**What the Minar proves, which is the examinable part.** It proves that a single "Sultanate monument" can be a layered, multi-generational patronage record. That is a method, not a fact about one tower, and it is reusable: it is the same analytical move that Topic 02 applies to Sanchi, and it is applicable to any long-construction monument in any topic. An answer that says "the Qutb Minar shows that monuments must be read as accumulations, not as single acts" has extracted the transferable point.

### 4. The Quwwat-ul-Islam mosque and the spolia question

The standard account, listing other monuments in the Qutb Minar premises, records the **Quwwat-ul-Islam mosque in the Qutb Minar premises, which was converted from a Jain temple**, and the **Adhai Din Ka Jhonpra in Ajmer**.

A more specific and better documented statement is available: the **Quwwat-ul-Islam mosque inscription records reuse of material from 27 demolished Hindu and Jaina temples**, as carried in the Archaeological Survey of India's record of the Qutb Minar complex. The analytical point follows: this is **specific evidence of architectural reuse or spolia in the Indo-Islamic corpus**, and a strong answer should **name this documented case rather than making a generalised, unsupported claim about temples being destroyed to build mosques across the period**.

**Handling this properly, in four moves.**

1. **Use the documented version.** "The mosque's inscription records reuse of material from twenty-seven demolished Hindu and Jaina temples" is a sentence with a documentary basis, a number and a source. "It was converted from a Jain temple" is a weaker and different claim, and the two are not interchangeable: one is about reused material assembled into a new building, the other about a single structure being repurposed. Record the discrepancy; use the documented version.
2. **State what it is evidence of.** It is evidence of continuity of building fabric across a religious and political transition - the physical stuff of earlier buildings entering a new one, rather than construction from a blank site. That is an architectural fact about how the building was made.
3. **State the boundary of the inference.** The discipline cuts both ways: do **not** dispose of the case by describing it as the conversion of one temple, and do **not** treat its spolia as representative of all Sultanate-period construction. A specific record supports a specific claim.
4. **Do not import a verdict this topic does not own.** The political and religious history of the period belongs to Medieval Indian History. What is owned here is the fabric, the inscription and the architectural consequence.

**Why the case is analytically valuable rather than merely uncomfortable.** Reused columns and dressed stone from earlier buildings carry their own carving, proportions and module. Assembling them into a new plan produces a building whose parts and whole disagree - which is exactly why the earliest Delhi work looks the way it does, and exactly why its screen is corbelled. The spolia record and the structural record explain each other.

### 5. Adhai Din Ka Jhonpra, and the third early monument

The standard account names **Adhai Din Ka Jhonpra in Ajmer** among the early examples, alongside the Quwwat-ul-Islam, without further description. That is all the record supplies, and nothing more is invented here.

Two disciplined things can still be said. First, its presence in the list establishes that early adaptation was **not confined to Delhi**: Ajmer is a second centre in the first phase. Second, the same building appears as one of the four options in a reproduced objective item asking where the first extant true arch is found - which places it inside the technical debate of Lesson 2 without settling anything, since the key printed beside that item selects the Quwwat-ul-Islam while the generally accepted attribution is the tomb of Balban.

### Criticism and reply: does the spolia evidence damage the synthesis argument?

**The objection.** If the first great mosque at Delhi was built from the material of demolished temples, then "synthesis" is a euphemism. What the evidence shows is appropriation, and describing it as a confluence of styles is an evasion.

**The reply, in three parts.** First, the objection is right that a synthesis claim must not be used to soften a documented fact, and no answer should do so. Second, the objection over-reads a single documented case into a general characterisation: that limit is exactly what the evidence imposes, and the twenty-seven-temple inscription is valuable **because** it is specific. Third - and this is the decisive point - the synthesis claim in this topic is not primarily a claim about intentions. It is a claim about **method**: imported arcuate knowledge executed by indigenous craftsmen in local stone, retaining lintel-and-beam construction and local motifs. That claim is supported by the whole subsequent record, from Bengal's roofs to Malwa's baulis to the lotus, bell and swastika on Sultanate and Mughal fabric, and it is not refuted by the circumstances of one early mosque.

**The balanced verdict.** "The early Delhi buildings record both a documented instance of reuse and the beginning of a genuinely hybrid construction method. An answer that reports only one of these is incomplete; an answer that generalises either into a claim about the whole period is unsupported."

### Examination use

This lesson supplies two things no other lesson does. The first is the **layered-monument method**, which is reusable across topics and which immediately upgrades any answer that mentions the Qutb Minar - the correct sentence names three reigns, not one. The second is the **calibrated handling of the spolia case**, which is the clearest opportunity in this topic to demonstrate evidentiary judgement: name the documented inscription, state what it shows, state what it does not show, and refuse both the sanitised and the generalised version. Examiners reward that precision far more than they reward an additional monument name.

### Revision notes

1. Slave or Ilbari dynasty, 1206-1290 AD; the style of the period is called the Mameluke style.
2. Method: most constructions were remodellings of existing Hindu structures, and the rulers also began erecting new monuments. Both halves belong in an answer.
3. Practical reason for early adaptation: immediate need for congregational space, available dressed stone and quarries, and masons trained in trabeate and corbelled technique.
4. Qutb Minar, first account: five storeys; initiated by Qutb-ud-Din Aibak, who built only the ground floor; next three storeys completed by Iltutmish; fifth storey built by Firoz Shah Tughlaq.
5. Qutb Minar, second account: begun by Aibak, substantially completed by Iltutmish, with Firoz Shah Tughlaq later repairing or rebuilding damaged upper work. The discrepancy is recorded, not resolved.
6. Transferable method: a single monument can be a layered, multi-generational patronage record - the same reading applied to Sanchi in Topic 02.
7. Quwwat-ul-Islam, standard textbook wording: in the Qutb Minar premises, "converted from a Jain temple".
8. Quwwat-ul-Islam, on the ASI record of the complex: the mosque inscription records reuse of material from 27 demolished Hindu and Jaina temples. This is the documented version and the one to use.
9. What the case shows: continuity of building fabric across a religious and political transition, rather than construction from a blank site.
10. What the case does not show: a general pattern of systematic destruction across the Sultanate period. Both over-readings - "just one temple converted" and "temples destroyed everywhere" - go beyond what the inscription will bear.
11. Adhai Din Ka Jhonpra, Ajmer, is the third named early monument; the record gives the name and location only, and establishes that early adaptation was not confined to Delhi.
12. Spolia explains structure: reused columns and dressed stone carry their own module and carving, which is part of why the earliest Delhi work reads as it does and why its great screen is corbelled.
13. The synthesis claim of this topic is a claim about method - imported technique, indigenous craft, local material, retained motifs - and is therefore not refuted by the circumstances of one building.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two sentences about the Quwwat-ul-Islam mosque are in circulation: "it was converted from a Jain temple" and "its inscription records the reuse of material from twenty-seven demolished Hindu and Jaina temples." Explain why these are not the same claim, and which an answer should use.

**Model answer:** They differ in object, in scale and in evidentiary status. The first is a claim about a single building being repurposed - one structure, changed in function. The second is a claim about materials drawn from many structures being assembled into a new building - a different architectural process, since a converted temple retains its own plan and proportions while a building made of reused material is newly planned around older parts. The second is also documented: it is attributed to the mosque's own inscription and carried in the Archaeological Survey of India's record of the complex, and it states a number. An answer should use the second, because it is specific, sourced and checkable, and should note the first only as the alternative formulation found in general study material. Crucially, using the documented version also fixes the limit of the inference: a record about one mosque's fabric supports a claim about that mosque, not about Sultanate construction generally.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that the safer course is to omit the reuse evidence altogether. Omission is not neutrality; it removes the single best-documented case of architectural continuity across a political transition and weakens the answer's evidentiary base. The discipline required is not silence but precision - name the documented instance, state what it demonstrates about building fabric, and refuse to extend it into a general characterisation of the period, which is exactly the boundary the inscription itself sets.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"A single monument can be a record of several reigns." Examine this proposition with reference to early Sultanate architecture at Delhi. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

The Qutb Minar is the standard demonstration. Its ground floor was built under Qutb-ud-Din Aibak; the next three storeys were carried out under Iltutmish, who substantially completed the tower; and the uppermost work is associated with Firoz Shah Tughlaq, described in one account as the building of the fifth storey and in another as the repair or rebuilding of damaged upper portions. More than a century and three patrons are therefore embedded in one structure.

The consequence is methodological. Attributing the monument to its initiator misstates the evidence and conceals the changes of material, proportion and workmanship between stages, which are themselves datable information.

The same reading applies to the adjacent Quwwat-ul-Islam mosque, whose fabric incorporates reused material recorded in its inscription.

Monuments are therefore best read as accumulations, with construction, extension and repair kept as separate dates.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the three-reign sequence stated accurately with the disputed third stage flagged rather than flattened 4 + the methodological consequence that attribution to the initiator misstates the evidence 2 + the point that stage changes in material and workmanship are themselves data 2 + a second monument extending the claim 1 + explicit separation of construction, extension and repair dates 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 7:** The first phase has been read closely. Now the sequence has to be walked: four imperial dynasties, each with its own material, its own structural preoccupation and its own building type - and one of them contributes a device that reshapes every later skyline in India.
