The table is built to be read in two directions. Across a row, it describes a phase. Down a column, it produces an argument - and the material column is the one that pays, because it establishes that **material as a datable marker** is a Sultanate habit before it becomes the celebrated Mughal one of Lesson 14.

### 1. The split that organises the whole period

The standard account divides Delhi Sultanate architecture, **1206-1526 AD**, into two broad categories: **Imperial Style**, patronised by the rulers of Delhi, and **Provincial Style**, patronised by local rulers and fiefs.

The members of each are standard: the imperial style covers the **Slave or Mameluke, Khilji or Seljuk, Tughlaq and Lodi** dynasties; the provincial style covers the **Bengal, Jaunpur or Sharqi, Malwa or Pathan, and Bijapur or Deccan** schools.

This is a **patronage classification**, and saying so out loud is worth marks. It is not a classification by date - the phases overlap heavily with the provincial schools - and it is not a classification by form, because several provincial schools use the imperial vocabulary and several imperial buildings use local devices. It is a classification by **who was paying and from where**, and that is precisely why it explains the variation: a Delhi sultan and a Bengal sultan face different climates, different materials, different labour traditions and different political needs.

The standard account also states the underlying unity plainly: within the imperial style, **each ruler imparted certain flavours of his own, but the broad stylisation remained the same.**

### 2. Slave or Mameluke, 1206-1290 AD

Covered in full in Lesson 6. To summarise for the sequence: the dynasty is also called **Ilbari**; the style is the **Mameluke style**; **most constructions were remodellings of existing Hindu structures**, alongside the erection of new monuments; the key works are the **Qutb Minar**, the **Quwwat-ul-Islam mosque** and **Adhai Din Ka Jhonpra at Ajmer**.

### 3. Khilji, 1290-1320 AD: the phase where the technology settles

The standard account records the Khiljis as ruling **from 1290 AD to 1320 AD** and establishing the **Seljuk style of architecture**. Three facts follow, and they belong together:

- constructions were **marked by the use of red sandstone**;
- **the prominence of the arcuate style began from this period**;
- **mortar began to be used prominently in all the constructions as a cementing agent**.

Named examples: the **Alai Darwaza by Alauddin Khilji** and **Siri Fort**.

**Why these three facts are one fact.** Arcuate construction at scale requires reliable bedding mortar; reliable mortar plus a consistent, workable stone makes repeatable arch and dome work possible; and a distinctive stone gives the whole phase a recognisable colour. The Khilji phase is where the Lesson 2 technology stops being exceptional and becomes normal practice - which is exactly the "industrialisation, not invention" formulation, located at a dynasty and a date. That is a considerably stronger sentence than "the Khiljis used red sandstone."

The **Alai Darwaza** is also, on the attribution recorded in Lesson 2, the building carrying the **earliest surviving true dome** in India, dated **1311**. That attribution is flagged again in the examination section, because a reproduced answer key on the related true-arch question points elsewhere.

### 4. Tughlaq: the phase that is described as a crisis and behaves like an engineering programme

The standard account is blunt: the Tughlaq period was **the crisis period for architecture during the Delhi Sultanate period**. But its own description of what was built contradicts the label's implication of decay. Recorded:

- **some construction works were still undertaken, which used grey sandstone**;
- **the focus was more on strength of the building, and so there was less emphasis on decoration**;
- **the constructions combined both arch and lintel method of entrance design**;
- they **introduced a style of construction known as batter, characterised by sloping walls to give more strength to the building**;
- examples: the **cities of Tughlaqabad, Jahanpanah and Ferozabad**.

**How to read this honestly.** "Crisis" here plainly means a reduction in ornamental ambition and in the number of prestige commissions; it does not mean technical regression. A phase that introduces a structural device still used centuries later, that standardises the combination of arch and lintel, and whose named products are three **cities** rather than three buildings, is not architecturally inert. The examinable formulation is: **the Tughlaq phase substitutes engineering and urban scale for decorative display.**

**Batter, explained.** A battered wall slopes inward as it rises. This lowers the wall's centre of gravity, increases the effective base width relative to the height, and improves resistance both to overturning and to the outward thrust delivered by arches and vaults inside. It is the direct structural response to the problem Lesson 2 named - arcuate construction pushes sideways - and it is why Tughlaq fabric looks heavy and tapering. It also travels: the Malwa school later uses **the batter system as introduced by the Tughlaqs** to strengthen its buildings, which is a documented case of an imperial device migrating into a provincial school.

### 5. Lodi: the phase that looks smallest and contributes most

The standard account records that **under the Lodi dynasty, architecture continued to take a backseat**, with **only tombs commissioned during this period**. Then it records the contribution: **one important feature of architecture during this period was the introduction of double domes**, consisting of **a hollow dome inside the top dome**, for two stated reasons - **to give strength to the structure** and **to lower the inner height of the dome**.

It also records the tomb type: **hard and bare, without any lavish decorations**, **built in octagonal shapes, with a diameter of roughly 15 m**, and **supported by a sloping verandah**. Examples: **Lodi Gardens**, and **the city of Agra, which was established by Sikandar Lodi**.

As Lesson 3 established, the double-dome claim should be refined to the **Lodi-Humayun transition**, with **Sikandar Lodi's tomb of 1517** as the early Indian case, and **Humayun's tomb** developing the device at monumental scale. No single inventor is to be named.

**The irony worth stating in an answer.** The phase whose architecture the standard account describes as having taken a backseat is the phase that hands the next three centuries their most consequential formal device. The narrative of decline and the record of technical contribution point in opposite directions, and noticing that is exactly the kind of observation that lifts a chronology into an argument.

### Comparative note: the four phases on the dimension that matters most

| Phase | What the phase was mainly solving | The evidence that shows it |
|---|---|---|
| Slave / Mameluke | How to get usable congregational and commemorative architecture immediately | Remodelling of existing structures; corbelled screen work; a tower begun and left to successors |
| Khilji | How to make the new technology routine | Arcuate prominence begins; mortar used prominently; a consistent red sandstone |
| Tughlaq | How to build strong and build large under constraint | Grey sandstone; strength over decoration; arch and lintel combined; batter; three cities |
| Lodi | How to build a small number of monuments that still command a skyline | Only tombs; bare octagonal type about 15 m across; the double dome introduced |

Notice that no row in the right-hand column is a list of names. Each is a problem plus its evidence, which is the form an examiner can reward.

### Examination use

This lesson is the spine of any **Sultanate development demand** and the necessary first half of any **Sultanate-versus-Mughal comparison**. Four specific moves are available. Use the **imperial-provincial split as a patronage classification**, and say so. Use the **material sequence** - reused stone, red sandstone, grey sandstone, bare stone - to show that material dating begins before the Mughals. Use the **Khilji phase** to locate the industrialisation argument at a dynasty and a date rather than leaving it abstract. And use the **Lodi double dome** to show that the narrative of decline and the record of technical contribution can point in opposite directions, which is a judgement an examiner cannot get from a list.

### Revision notes

1. Delhi Sultanate architecture, 1206-1526 AD, splits into Imperial Style (patronised by the rulers of Delhi) and Provincial Style (patronised by local rulers and fiefs).
2. Imperial members: Slave or Mameluke, Khilji or Seljuk, Tughlaq, Lodi. Provincial members: Bengal, Jaunpur or Sharqi, Malwa or Pathan, Bijapur or Deccan.
3. This is a patronage classification, not a chronological or formal one - which is why it explains variation.
4. Within the imperial style, each ruler imparted his own flavours but the broad stylisation remained the same.
5. Slave or Ilbari, 1206-1290, Mameluke style: mostly remodelling of existing structures, plus new monuments; Qutb Minar, Quwwat-ul-Islam, Adhai Din Ka Jhonpra.
6. Khilji, 1290-1320, Seljuk style: red sandstone; the prominence of the arcuate style begins here; mortar begins to be used prominently as a cementing agent; Alai Darwaza by Alauddin Khilji and Siri Fort.
7. The three Khilji facts are one fact: mortar plus consistent stone plus arcuate prominence equals repeatable arch and dome work - the industrialisation argument, dated.
8. Alai Darwaza, 1311, carries the earliest surviving true dome on the generally accepted attribution; a reproduced key on the related true-arch item points elsewhere, and both are recorded.
9. Tughlaq, described as the crisis period: grey sandstone; strength emphasised over decoration; arch and lintel combined in entrance design; batter introduced - sloping walls for strength; cities of Tughlaqabad, Jahanpanah and Ferozabad.
10. Batter explained: an inward-sloping wall lowers the centre of gravity, widens the effective base and resists the outward thrust of arches and vaults. Later reused by the Malwa school, explicitly credited to the Tughlaqs.
11. Correct reading of "crisis": reduced ornamental ambition and fewer prestige commissions, not technical regression. The phase substitutes engineering and urban scale for display.
12. Lodi: architecture takes a backseat; only tombs commissioned; double dome introduced - a hollow dome inside the top dome - for strength and to lower the inner height; tombs hard and bare, octagonal, about 15 m in diameter, with a sloping verandah; Lodi Gardens and the city of Agra founded by Sikandar Lodi.
13. Refinement: mature double dome at the Lodi-Humayun transition; Sikandar Lodi's tomb of 1517 is the early Indian case; no inventor to be named.
14. The material column read downward - reused stone, red sandstone, grey sandstone, bare stone - shows material dating as a Sultanate practice, before the Mughal red-to-white sequence.

### Concept check

**Question:** The standard account calls the Tughlaq period a "crisis period for architecture" and says architecture "took a backseat" under the Lodis. Using only what those same passages record as built, explain why neither label should be reproduced without qualification.

**Model answer:** Both labels describe patronage volume and ornamental ambition, not technical capacity, and the passages themselves supply the correction. The Tughlaq entry records grey sandstone construction, an explicit shift of emphasis from decoration to structural strength, the combination of arch and lintel in entrance design, and the introduction of batter - a genuine structural innovation later adopted by the Malwa school and credited there to the Tughlaqs. Its named products are three cities, which is the largest scale at which this tradition operates. The Lodi entry records that only tombs were commissioned, yet in the same breath records the introduction of the double dome, the device that separates external silhouette from internal height and underpins every later monumental Mughal mausoleum. So the qualified formulation is that both phases reduced decorative display and prestige commissions while making durable technical contributions, and an answer that repeats "crisis" and "backseat" flatly has reported the label while ignoring the evidence printed beside it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a period label as a finding. Labels such as crisis, decline, backseat and golden age are summaries of patronage, and they frequently contradict the technical record in the same source. The habit to build is to ask, for every period label encountered in this topic, what was actually built and what device was actually added - the same discipline that Lesson 16 will require for the word "decline" under Aurangzeb.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Trace the development of imperial Delhi Sultanate architecture, showing how material and structural preoccupation changed by dynasty. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

Imperial Sultanate architecture, patronised by the rulers of Delhi between 1206 and 1526, moves through four phases with distinct materials and priorities.

Under the Slave or Mameluke dynasty, construction was largely remodelling of existing structures using available stone, alongside new work such as the Qutb Minar, begun by Aibak and substantially carried up by Iltutmish.

The Khiljis, in red sandstone, made the arcuate system prominent and began using mortar prominently as a cementing agent, which converted arch and dome work into repeatable practice; the Alai Darwaza and Siri Fort belong here.

The Tughlaqs used grey sandstone, subordinated decoration to strength, combined arch with lintel and introduced batter - sloping walls - while building the cities of Tughlaqabad, Jahanpanah and Ferozabad.

The Lodis commissioned only tombs, bare and octagonal, but introduced the double dome.

Material therefore tracks ambition and technique together.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = all four phases in order with correct dynastic names 3 + the material sequence red sandstone to grey sandstone to bare stone used as the organising thread 2 + mortar and arcuate prominence attributed to the Khiljis 2 + batter attributed to the Tughlaqs with its purpose 1 + the double dome attributed to the Lodi phase 1 + at least four named works distributed across phases 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 8:** The imperial half of the classification is complete. The provincial half is where the topic becomes genuinely interesting, because the same structural vocabulary is now handed to builders working in different rain, different stone and different politics. The first of them roofs its mosques like its huts - and Delhi will eventually copy it back.
## Lesson 8 - Bengal: brick, basalt, and the roof that travelled back to Delhi

Progress: 8/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Bengal school, its material logic in a stoneless delta, the bangaldar roof as a translated hut form, and the return traffic into Mughal architecture

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Bengal school passage, its material and its roof form
CA search: "Not required - provincial school lesson"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: how a delta decides a building

```text
  THE GEOGRAPHICAL PREMISE                     THE ARCHITECTURAL RESULT

  alluvial delta - deep silt,     ------->   BRICK becomes the structural
  no building stone at hand                  material (fired from the same
                                             alluvium the delta supplies)

  hard stone must be brought in   ------->   BLACK BASALT used selectively:
  from outside the delta                     columns, dressings, facings -
                                             the expensive material is spent
                                             where it shows or where it works
                                             hardest

  very heavy monsoon rainfall     ------->   the roof must SHED water fast
                                             and must not pond at the ridge

  existing local solution:                   the CURVED BAMBOO-AND-THATCH
  the village hut                 ------->   hut roof, whose ridge and eaves
                                             sag into a downward curve

                 THE TRANSLATION INTO MASONRY

          thatch + bamboo                    brick + basalt
          curved ridge  ----------------->   curved cornice and roofline
          sagging eaves ----------------->   "BANGLA" / BANGALDAR ROOF
                                             (do-chala: two slopes;
                                              chau-chala: four slopes)

                              |
                              v
        used first on TEMPLES in the region, then continued on MOSQUES
                              |
                              v
        LATER BORROWED BY MUGHAL ARCHITECTS  -> the device returns
        upstream, into the imperial repertoire
```

The diagram is a causal chain, and it should be read as one: no stone, therefore brick; heavy rain, therefore a shedding roof; an existing local roof form, therefore translation rather than invention; and then, because the form is good, adoption by the very tradition that had arrived from outside. That last arrow is the one that makes Bengal the most valuable single school in this topic.

### 1. What the record says

The standard account gives the **Bengal School** the dates **1203-1573 AD** and records three things:

- it was **characterised by its use of bricks and black basalts**;
- the **mosques built during this period continued the use of sloping "Bangla roofs", which was previously used for temples**;
- the named examples are the **Qadam Rasul Mosque** and the **Adina Mosque in Malda district of West Bengal**, with a separate caption in the same section recording a **group of monuments in Gaur and Pandua**.

Two further points complete the picture: Bengal's **sloping "Bangla roof"** was **later borrowed by Mughal architects**, and its drivers were **heavy rainfall** and the translation of a **local hut form into masonry**, with the material **locally sourced brick and basalt**.

**A note on location.** The standard account names both the Qadam Rasul and the Adina mosques as being in Malda district, and separately captions a group of monuments in Gaur and Pandua. Both statements are reproduced here as given, without silently correcting or expanding either, since no further locational detail is on record.

### 2. The material argument, made properly

This is the lesson where "material determines form" stops being a slogan and becomes a demonstration.

**Why brick.** The Bengal delta is built of silt. There is no workable building stone in the deltaic plain, and transporting stone across a landscape of rivers and inundation is expensive and slow. But the same silt fires into excellent brick, and brick is made where it is used. So the structural material is chosen by geology, not by taste.

**What brick does to form.** Brick is a small unit. It is superb for curved surfaces, because a curve can be approximated by many small pieces; it is poor for long spans as a beam; and its surface wants either plaster or moulded and carved ornament rather than deep sculptural relief. A brick tradition therefore tends towards **many small openings rather than a few large ones**, towards **curved and moulded profiles**, and towards **terracotta surface ornament**.

**Why basalt, and where.** **Black basalt** is hard, dense, takes a fine finish and must be brought in from outside the delta. It is therefore used selectively - in columns, dressings and facings - where hardness or visual contrast is worth the cost. The contrast of dark stone against brick is itself an architectural effect.

**What this proves for the topic's central argument.** A mosque in Bengal and a mosque in Delhi of the same century are built to the same liturgical specification - the qibla wall, the mihrab, the covered hall - and look substantially different, because the specification is constant while material and climate are not. That single comparison is a complete refutation of the idea that Indo-Islamic architecture is a monolithic imported style. Keep it ready; it is the fastest way to open a regional-variation answer.

### 3. The bangaldar roof, and why it is worth a paragraph

The standard account's formulation is precise and should be kept: Bengal's mosques **continued** the use of sloping Bangla roofs **which were previously used for temples**. Two claims are packed into that clause.

- **Continuity across religion.** The same roof form served temples and then mosques. The builders did not change the roof when the patron changed, because the rain did not change.
- **Continuity across material.** The form originates in bamboo-and-thatch village building, where the ridge and eaves naturally sag into a curve, and it is then translated into brick and stone, where the curve has to be deliberately constructed.

The family terminology is worth holding: **bangaldar** names the curved roof form, **do-chala** a two-sided sloping roof and **chau-chala** a four-sided one, and Mughal adoption of these Bengal roof forms became significant in later north Indian building. Only the "Bangla roof" itself is named in the standard account; the rest of the family is supplied here.

**Why the curve, functionally.** A curved roof that is steep at the ridge and flattens towards the eaves sheds water quickly where accumulation is most dangerous and then discharges it clear of the wall. In a bamboo structure the curve arises from the material's natural sag; in masonry it must be built, which means it is being retained for performance and for its familiar appearance, not because it is easy.

### 4. The return traffic, which is the examinable climax

An imported structural technology reached Bengal. Bengal solved it with local material and a local roof. And then **Mughal architects borrowed the Bengal roof**.

This single fact does more argumentative work than any other in the provincial section:

- it **breaks the direction of influence**, showing the traffic was not one-way from an imperial centre to passive provinces;
- it **defeats essentialism concretely**, because the borrowed element originates in a village vernacular used on temples before mosques and is adopted by the most prestigious imperial patrons in India;
- it **converts a regional curiosity into a national argument**, which is what a 15- or 20-mark answer needs.

The sentence to keep is short: **"Bengal roofed its mosques the way it roofed its huts, and the Mughals then roofed their pavilions the way Bengal roofed its mosques."**

### Comparative note: Bengal against the imperial Delhi phases

| Dimension | Imperial Delhi | Bengal |
|---|---|---|
| Structural material | Dressed stone - reused stone, then red sandstone, then grey sandstone | Brick, with black basalt used selectively |
| Roof and skyline | Dome and, from the Lodis, the double dome | Curved bangaldar roof, sloping, derived from the hut |
| Ornament method | Carved stone, calligraphy, arabesque, later inlay | Moulded and carved brick and terracotta, with basalt contrast |
| Driver of form | Imperial display and the available imperial quarry | Rainfall, absence of local stone and an existing vernacular |
| Direction of influence | Outward, as the imperial model | Inward, then outward again when the Mughals borrow the roof |

The last row is the one to underline. Bengal is the only school on record as exporting a device back into the imperial tradition.

### Examination use

Bengal is the strongest single opening example for a **regional-variation demand**, because the causal chain is short, complete and checkable: no local stone, so brick; heavy rain, so a shedding roof; an existing hut roof, so translation. It is also the strongest closing example for a **synthesis demand**, because of the return traffic into Mughal architecture. And it is the correct answer to the trap question "was Indo-Islamic architecture an imported template?", because a template does not acquire the roof of the place it lands in and then carry that roof back to the capital.

### Revision notes

1. Bengal School, 1203-1573 AD, one of the four provincial schools alongside Jaunpur, Malwa and Bijapur.
2. Characterised by the use of bricks and black basalts.
3. Mosques continued the use of sloping "Bangla roofs", previously used for temples.
4. Named examples: Qadam Rasul Mosque and Adina Mosque, recorded by the standard account as being in Malda district of West Bengal; the same section separately captions a group of monuments in Gaur and Pandua.
5. Material logic: the delta has no building stone but excellent brick clay, so brick is structural and imported black basalt is selective - columns, dressings, facings.
6. Brick's consequences for form: many small openings rather than a few large ones; curved and moulded profiles; surface ornament in moulded brick and terracotta rather than deep relief.
7. The bangaldar roof originates in curved bamboo-and-thatch hut construction and is translated into masonry, where the curve must be deliberately built.
8. Terminology: bangaldar for the curved form, do-chala for a two-sided and chau-chala for a four-sided sloping roof; Mughal adoption of these forms became significant in later north Indian building.
9. Function of the curve: sheds water fast at the ridge and discharges it clear of the wall - a rainfall response, not a decorative preference.
10. The decisive fact: the Bengal sloping roof was later borrowed by Mughal architects, so influence travelled upstream as well as downstream.
11. The comparison that refutes monolithic readings: a Bengal mosque and a Delhi mosque of the same century meet the same liturgical specification and look substantially different, because material and climate differ while the specification does not.
12. Usable one-liner: Bengal roofed its mosques the way it roofed its huts, and the Mughals then roofed their pavilions the way Bengal roofed its mosques.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Bengal school is better evidence against the "imported template" view of Indo-Islamic architecture than any amount of general statement about synthesis.

**Model answer:** Because it supplies a complete causal chain and a documented reversal, both of which can be checked. The chain runs from geology to form without any interpretive step: the delta yields no building stone but excellent brick clay, so the structural material is brick, with hard black basalt imported selectively for columns and dressings; heavy monsoon rainfall requires a roof that sheds quickly; the region already possessed such a roof in its curved bamboo-and-thatch hut, used on its temples; and that curved roof was translated into masonry and carried over to mosques. Nothing in this sequence is imported except the liturgical requirement. The reversal then closes the argument: the sloping Bengal roof was later borrowed by Mughal architects, so the device moved from a village vernacular to a regional sultanate to the imperial repertoire. A template does not acquire the roof of the place it lands in and then export that roof to the capital.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating provincial schools as diluted or provincial versions of an imperial standard. The evidence runs the other way. Bengal solves a genuine engineering problem that Delhi does not face, in a material Delhi does not use, with a form Delhi eventually adopts. The correct framing is that provincial schools are parallel solutions to a shared specification, not degraded copies of a central one.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Provincial architecture is the proof that Indo-Islamic form was never an imported template." Examine this with reference to Bengal. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

Bengal supports the claim through material, climate and a documented reversal of influence.

The delta offers no building stone, so the Bengal school, dated 1203-1573, built in brick, reserving imported black basalt for columns and dressings. Brick is a small unit suited to curved profiles and moulded surface ornament, which already distinguishes Bengal work from the dressed stone of imperial Delhi.

Climate did the rest. Heavy rainfall demands a roof that sheds fast, and the region possessed one in the curved bamboo-and-thatch hut roof already used on its temples. That roof was translated into masonry and continued on mosques as the sloping Bangla roof, at monuments such as the Adina and Qadam Rasul mosques.

The decisive evidence is the return traffic: Mughal architects later borrowed this Bengal roof.

A template does not adopt the vernacular of its destination and then export it upstream.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the geological premise with brick and selective basalt 3 + the climatic premise with the hut-to-masonry translation 3 + the temple-to-mosque continuity of the roof form 1 + the Mughal borrowing stated as the decisive reversal 2 + named monuments and the school's dates 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 9:** Bengal varied the roof. The next two schools vary something more surprising - they drop an element that the imperial account treats as defining. Two provincial schools build no minars at all, and one of them turns climate control into a design programme.

## Lesson 9 - Jaunpur and Malwa: the screen, the refused minar, and architecture as climate control

Progress: 9/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Sharqi screen facade and the absence of minars, the Malwa or Pathan school's coloured stone and stairs, and its five recorded devices of environmental adaptation

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Jaunpur and Malwa school passages, including the environmental-adaptation list
CA search: "Not required - provincial school lesson"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two schools, two refusals, one shared absence

```text
  JAUNPUR (SHARQI), 1394-1479 AD           MALWA (PATHAN), 1405-1569 AD
  patrons: the Sharqi rulers               centres: Dhar and Mandu

  +--------------------------------+       +--------------------------------+
  |  THE FACADE IS THE STATEMENT   |       |  THE BUILDING IS A CLIMATE     |
  |                                |       |  MACHINE                       |
  |   [ M A S S I V E   S C R E E N ]      |                                |
  |   ______________________________|      |  1. LARGE WINDOWS              |
  |  |  bold and forceful characters |     |     -> rooms well ventilated   |
  |  |  PAINTED on huge screens in   |     |                                |
  |  |  the CENTRE and SIDE BAYS of  |     |  2. LIGHTLY ARCHED PAVILIONS   |
  |  |  the prayer hall              |     |     -> airy, stay cool in heat |
  |  |______________________________ |     |                                |
  |                                |       |  3. BAULIS (artificial         |
  |   and NO MINARS                |       |     reservoirs) -> water       |
  |                                |       |     storage in the premises    |
  |   example: ATALA MOSQUE,       |       |                                |
  |            Jaunpur             |       |  4. LOCAL MATERIALS            |
  +--------------------------------+       |                                |
                                           |  5. BATTER, as introduced by   |
  SHARED ABSENCE:                          |     the Tughlaqs -> strength   |
  the standard account records that        |                                |
  the Sharqi style "also avoided           |  plus: DIFFERENT COLOURED      |
  the use of minars, AS THE                |  STONES AND MARBLES; stairs    |
  PATHAN STYLE" - i.e. Malwa               |  used to enhance aesthetics;   |
                                           |  arches and pillars stylised;  |
  -> "minars are universal in              |  and NO MINARS                 |
     Indo-Islamic architecture"            |                                |
     is FALSE, and these two               |  examples: RANI ROOPMATI       |
     schools are the counter-              |  PAVILION, JAHAZ MAHAL,        |
     evidence                              |  ASHRAFI MAHAL, HINDOLA MAHAL  |
                                           +--------------------------------+
```

Two schools, in different regions, under different dynasties, independently decline to build the element that the general account treats as one of the defining introductions of the period. That is not an oversight in the record; it is the record telling you that the "defining features" list is a description of imperial Delhi, not a law of the tradition.

### 1. Jaunpur, or the Sharqi school

The standard account records the **Jaunpur School, 1394-1479 AD**, **patronised by the Sharqi rulers**, under whom **Jaunpur became a centre of great art and cultural activity**. Three features follow:

- the style **also came to be known as Sharqi style**;
- it **avoided the use of minars, as the Pathan style**;
- **a unique feature of the buildings here is the use of bold and forceful characters painted on huge screens in the centre and side bays of the prayer hall.**

The named example is the **Atala Mosque, Jaunpur**.

**What the screen is doing, architecturally.** Recall the mosque plan of Lesson 4: the prayer hall sits against the qibla wall and faces the courtyard, and its courtyard-facing front is the building's principal public face. Most traditions articulate that front with a pishtaq - a tall frame around the central arch. The Sharqi solution is to expand that frame into a **massive screen across the centre and the side bays**, so the whole front of the prayer hall becomes a single dominating wall of masonry carrying bold lettering.

The consequence is a distinctive silhouette in which **the screen replaces the minar as the element that makes the building visible and imposing from a distance**. This is worth stating as a substitution rather than an absence: Jaunpur did not simply omit a feature, it solved the same problem - monumental presence - with a different device.

**The driver, stated as an inference.** The Jaunpur device is best summarised as **monumental screen facades with no minars**, with the driver read as the **political self-assertion of a regional sultanate**, at the **Atala Mosque**. That political reading is an inference, but a defensible one: a regional sultanate building in deliberate distinction from Delhi has a reason to reject Delhi's most conspicuous marker and to invent its own.

### 2. Malwa, or the Pathan school

The standard account records the **Malwa School, 1405-1569 AD**, centred on **the cities of Dhar and Mandu in the Malwa plateau**. Its features:

- **the most prominent feature of the buildings here was the use of different coloured stones and marbles**;
- **the buildings had large windows, which could be a result of European influence, and were decorated by stylised use of arches and pillars**;
- **even the stairs were used to enhance the aesthetics of the construction**;
- **minars were not used in this school of architecture**;
- it is **also known as the Pathan school of architecture**.

And then the passage that makes Malwa the single most quotable provincial school: it is **one of the finest specimens of environmental adaptation of the period**, for five recorded reasons:

1. **the use of large windows made the buildings and rooms well ventilated**;
2. **the pavilions were lightly arched which made them airy and allowed the buildings to remain cool during the heat**;
3. **artificial reservoirs known as baulis were constructed in the premises for storage of water**;
4. **locally available materials were used**;
5. **the use of the batter system as introduced by the Tughlaqs made the buildings strong**.

Named examples: **Rani Roopmati Pavilion**, **Jahaz Mahal** and **Ashrafi Mahal**, with **Hindola Mahal, Mandu** appearing in the same section.

**Read the list as a design programme, not as five facts.** Items one and two are **ventilation and passive cooling**; item three is **water storage**; item four is **supply-chain economy**; item five is **structural stability**, borrowed from the imperial centre. Together they describe a building type designed for a hot plateau where water must be held on site - and they describe it in the source's own words, which is unusual and valuable.

**The European-influence clause needs care.** The standard account writes that the large windows **could be a result of European influence**. That modal verb belongs to the original and must be preserved. Report the possibility as a suggestion rather than a finding, and note that the same passage immediately supplies a sufficient internal explanation - ventilation in heat. An answer that converts "could be" into "was" has manufactured a certainty the evidence does not contain.

**A boundary note on the two names.** The standard account calls the Malwa school "also known as the Pathan school of architecture," and separately says the Sharqi style avoided minars "as the Pathan style." Read together, the two sentences identify the Pathan style with Malwa. These lessons therefore use "Malwa or Pathan" throughout and do not treat "Pathan" as a separate fifth school.

### 3. Why the shared refusal of the minar matters so much

It belongs explicitly among this topic's traps: **"minars were a feature of every provincial school" is false - Jaunpur's Sharqi style and the Malwa or Pathan school both explicitly avoided minars.**

Three consequences for answering:

- **It falsifies the feature-list method.** A candidate who has memorised "arches, domes, minars, jaali, charbagh, calligraphy" as the definition of Indo-Islamic architecture will apply it to two entire schools that do not fit. The feature list describes imperial Delhi practice and must be presented as such.
- **It is a ready-made qualification.** Any answer asserting common features across the tradition can be strengthened in one clause: "though minars, often listed as a defining introduction, are absent from both the Sharqi and the Malwa schools."
- **It demonstrates that provincial choices were choices.** Two schools, independently, declined an available and prestigious element. That is a decision, and a decision invites explanation - regional self-assertion in Jaunpur's case, on the reading given above.

### Comparative note: three provincial schools so far, on four dimensions

| Dimension | Bengal, 1203-1573 | Jaunpur (Sharqi), 1394-1479 | Malwa (Pathan), 1405-1569 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Material | Brick with selective black basalt | Not separately specified on record | Different coloured stones and marbles; local materials |
| Signature device | Sloping bangaldar roof from the hut | Massive screen across the centre and side bays, with bold painted characters | Large ventilating windows, lightly arched pavilions, baulis, decorative stairs |
| Minar | Not recorded as a distinguishing issue | Explicitly avoided | Explicitly avoided |
| Driver | Rainfall and the absence of local stone | Regional political self-assertion | Heat and water storage on a plateau |

**An honest gap.** The record specifies Bengal's and Malwa's materials but does not separately specify Jaunpur's, and the gap is not filled here by inference.

### Examination use

These two schools are where a **regional-variation demand** becomes a genuine argument rather than a list. Use Jaunpur for the **substitution point** - a different solution to the same problem of monumental presence - and Malwa for the **environmental-adaptation programme**, which can be quoted in five items straight from the record and is the single most efficient block of evidence in the provincial section. Use the shared refusal of the minar as the **qualification** that upgrades any generalisation about common features. And treat the European-influence clause as a small, visible test of source discipline: reproduce the modality, do not harden it.

### Revision notes

1. Jaunpur School, 1394-1479 AD, patronised by the Sharqi rulers; Jaunpur became a centre of great art and cultural activity; the style is also called Sharqi style.
2. Jaunpur's unique recorded feature: bold and forceful characters painted on huge screens in the centre and side bays of the prayer hall.
3. Jaunpur avoided minars, "as the Pathan style" - so the screen, not the minar, carries monumental presence. Named example: Atala Mosque, Jaunpur.
4. The driver, read as an inference: political self-assertion of a regional sultanate.
5. Malwa School, 1405-1569 AD, centred on Dhar and Mandu in the Malwa plateau; also called the Pathan school.
6. Malwa's most prominent feature: the use of different coloured stones and marbles.
7. Malwa's large windows, which the source says could be a result of European influence - modality to be preserved, not hardened; the same passage supplies ventilation as a sufficient internal explanation.
8. Stairs used to enhance the aesthetics of the construction; arches and pillars used in stylised decoration; minars not used.
9. Malwa's five recorded devices of environmental adaptation: large windows for ventilation; lightly arched pavilions for airiness and coolness in heat; baulis, artificial reservoirs, for water storage in the premises; locally available materials; and batter, as introduced by the Tughlaqs, for strength.
10. Read as a programme: ventilation and passive cooling, water storage, supply-chain economy, structural stability.
11. Named Malwa examples: Rani Roopmati Pavilion, Jahaz Mahal, Ashrafi Mahal; Hindola Mahal, Mandu, appears in the same section.
12. Batter is a documented migration of an imperial Tughlaq device into a provincial school.
13. Trap defeated: minars are not universal. Two whole schools explicitly avoided them, which falsifies the feature-list definition of Indo-Islamic architecture.
14. Honest gap: the record does not separately specify Jaunpur's building material, and none is inferred here.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes that "the minar is one of the defining features of Indo-Islamic architecture, found in mosques and mausoleums throughout the period." Correct this with evidence, and explain what the correction reveals about how the standard feature list should be used.

**Model answer:** The claim is false as stated. The Jaunpur or Sharqi school, active from 1394 to 1479, avoided minars altogether and achieved monumental presence instead through massive screens across the centre and side bays of the prayer hall, as at the Atala Mosque; the Malwa or Pathan school, from 1405 to 1569, also did not use minars, and the standard account links the two refusals explicitly. Since both are provincial schools of the same period, the minar cannot be a defining feature of the tradition; it is a feature of imperial Delhi practice that was introduced around mosques and mausoleums there. What the correction reveals is that the standard feature list - arch, dome, minar, jali, charbagh, calligraphy - is a description of one patronage stream, and should be introduced as such and then immediately tested against the provincial schools. Used that way it is a framework; used as a definition it produces demonstrably wrong sentences.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that an absence in a provincial school is an omission or a deficiency. Jaunpur did not fail to build minars; it substituted a different and more emphatic device for the same purpose. Reading absence as substitution rather than as lack is the habit that turns a list of regional peculiarities into an argument about design choices, which is what the higher marks require.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The Malwa school is the clearest case of climate-responsive design in medieval Indian architecture." Discuss. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

The Malwa or Pathan school, active from 1405 to 1569 around Dhar and Mandu on the Malwa plateau, is recorded as one of the finest specimens of environmental adaptation of the period, and the evidence is explicit rather than inferred.

Five devices are recorded together. Large windows ventilated the buildings and rooms; lightly arched pavilions were airy and kept interiors cool in heat; artificial reservoirs called baulis stored water within the premises; locally available materials were used; and the batter system introduced by the Tughlaqs gave structural strength. Rani Roopmati Pavilion, Jahaz Mahal, Ashrafi Mahal and Hindola Mahal carry the idiom.

Taken together these address heat, water scarcity, supply cost and stability - a coherent programme rather than isolated features.

One caution: the suggestion that the large windows reflect European influence is offered in the source as a possibility, and should be reported as such.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = all five recorded adaptation devices 4 + the reading of them as a single programme addressing heat, water, supply and stability 2 + the school's dates, centres and alternative name 1 + at least two named monuments 1 + the batter attribution to the Tughlaqs as a migrating imperial device 1 + preservation of the European-influence modality 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 10:** Two schools have varied the roof and refused the tower. The next school takes the element everyone associates with this architecture - the dome - and pushes it further than anyone else in India, while roofing a vast hall in a way that appears to defy support.

## Lesson 10 - The Deccan: Adil Shahi Bijapur, the near-spherical dome and a ceiling with no visible support

Progress: 10/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Bijapur or Deccan school, the three-arched facade, the bulbous dome with its narrow neck, cornices, the unsupported-ceiling technique, and the whispering gallery with one printed slip corrected

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the Bijapur school passage and the whispering-gallery note beside it
CA search: "Not required - provincial school lesson"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the Deccan dome, and the problem it solves differently

```text
   HEMISPHERICAL DOME                  BIJAPUR'S BULBOUS DOME
   (the older profile)                 "almost spherical with a narrow neck"

          ___________                          _______________
       __/           \__                    _/                 \_
     _/                 \_                _/                     \_
    /                     \              /                         \
   |                       |            |                           |
   |                       |             \                         /
   +-----------------------+              \_                     _/
   |      drum / wall      |                \___             ___/
   |                       |                    \___     ___/
                                                     | | |   <- NARROW NECK
                                              +------| |-------+
                                              |   drum / wall  |

   the curve STOPS at the                the curve CONTINUES PAST the
   half-sphere; the widest               half-sphere; the widest point is
   point is at the springing             ABOVE the springing, and the dome
                                         is then gathered into a constricted
                                         neck where it meets the drum

   EFFECT: lower, heavier, calmer        EFFECT: taller, more dramatic,
                                         reads as a swelling mass set on a
                                         slender waist - visible far away

   THE BIJAPUR FACADE AND CEILING

   +---------------------------------------------------------+
   |          [ dome ]         [ dome ]        [ dome ]       |   <- corner
   |   ____________________________________________________   |      towers /
   |  |        |              |              |            |  |      finials
   |  |  ARCH  |     ARCH     |     ARCH     |            |  |
   |  |        |              |              |            |  |   THREE-ARCHED
   |  |________|______________|______________|____________|  |   FACADE
   |         cornice  ------------------------------------   |
   +---------------------------------------------------------+
   |                                                         |
   |   INTERIOR:  the ceiling is treated "WITHOUT ANY        |
   |   APPARENT SUPPORT" - iron clamps and a strong plaster  |
   |   of mortar give the structure its strength             |
   |                                                         |
   +---------------------------------------------------------+
```

The two dome profiles are the whole lesson in one image. Carrying the curve past the half-sphere and then pulling it into a neck is a deliberate and technically demanding choice, and it produces the silhouette that makes a Deccan skyline unmistakable.

### 1. The record

The standard account gives the **Bijapur School, 1490-1656 AD**, developing **under the patronage of Adil Shah**, and calls it **Bijapur style or the Deccan style of architecture**. Recorded features:

- Adil Shah **constructed a number of mosques, tombs and palaces**;
- these were **unique in the use of a three-arched facade and bulbous dome**, the domes being **almost spherical with a narrow neck**;
- he **also introduced the use of cornices**;
- **a special feature of the Bijapur school was the treatment of its ceilings, which were without any apparent support**;
- **iron clamps and a strong plaster of mortar were used to give strength to the buildings**;
- **the walls were decorated by rich carvings**.

The named example is **Gol Gumbaj, the mausoleum of Adil Shah, in Bijapur or Vijayapura**.

The same section carries a box on the **whispering gallery in India**, described as **made in such a manner that even whispers can be clearly heard in other parts of the gallery**, and listing three examples: **Gol Gumbaj**, the **Victoria Memorial**, and the **Golghar Granary in Bandipore**.

**A slip in the printed list.** The third whispering-gallery example is printed as "Golghar Granary in Bandipore." The Golghar granary is at Bankipore in Patna, Bihar. Both the printed form and the correction are recorded here, neither is used as an examinable fact beyond the whispering-gallery list itself, and the Victoria Memorial entry in the same list belongs to Topic 05's colonial material and appears here only as a cross-reference.

### 2. The dome, and why "almost spherical with a narrow neck" is the phrase to keep

Return to Lesson 3. A hemispherical dome stops its curve at the half-sphere, so the widest point of the dome is at the springing where it meets its support. A **bulbous** dome continues the curve past the half-sphere, so the widest point is **above** the springing, and the shell must then be brought back inward to meet the drum - which produces the **narrow neck**.

**Why this is structurally interesting rather than merely decorative.** Carrying a masonry shell past the half-sphere means the lower part of the shell is no longer purely in compression along its meridians; the overhanging mass wants to spread, and the neck is where the load is concentrated most sharply. Building this reliably requires confident mortar work, careful shaping and, where available, tying. The standard account's next sentence supplies exactly that: **iron clamps and a strong plaster of mortar were used to give strength to the buildings.** Read the two sentences together and the school's ambition and its means are stated in a single breath.

**Why it is visually decisive.** A dome that swells above a narrow waist reads from a great distance as a single object hovering over a city. It is the most efficient silhouette in this whole tradition for making one building dominate a horizon, and that is why the Deccan sultanates, which were competing regional powers, invested in it.

### 3. The three-arched facade and the cornice

**The three-arched facade** is the Deccan's answer to the same problem Jaunpur answered with a screen and Delhi with a pishtaq: how to give a building front a legible, monumental order. Three arches produce a symmetrical composition with an emphatic centre, and they scale well - the composition works on a mosque front, on a palace and on a tomb.

**The cornice**, recorded as Adil Shah's introduction, is a projecting horizontal band running along the top of a wall. Architecturally it does three things: it throws rainwater clear of the wall face; it casts a strong horizontal shadow that visually terminates the wall; and it creates a firm base line against which the domes above can be read. In a hot, bright climate the shadow line is not a small matter - it is what gives the elevation its crispness. The same element reappears, shallower, in the Sikh idiom of Lesson 18, and in a bowed form in Rajput work in Lesson 17, which makes the cornice a useful thread to follow across schools.

### 4. The ceiling "without any apparent support"

This is the most intriguing sentence in the Bijapur record, and it needs both enthusiasm and discipline.

**What is recorded**: the treatment of ceilings **without any apparent support**, with **iron clamps and a strong plaster of mortar** used to give the buildings strength.

**What that means architecturally**: a large roofed space in which no columns interrupt the floor and the ceiling appears to spring from the walls alone. The word doing the work is **apparent**. There is of course support - the walls, the transition zone, and whatever internal buttressing or interlocking the masons devised - but it is not visible as a forest of piers. Achieving this at Deccan scale is precisely the payoff of the arcuate system described in Lesson 2, taken to its limit.

**What is not recorded, and therefore not claimed here**: the specific geometry by which it was achieved. No construction method is supplied here that the record does not contain. The honest formulation for an answer is: "the Bijapur school is recorded as treating its ceilings without apparent support, using iron clamps and strong mortar plaster for strength" - which is both accurate and impressive, and which avoids inventing a technique.

### 5. Naming and place discipline

Three small points that cost marks when mishandled:

- the city is recorded as **Bijapur or Vijayapura**; both names are current, and either may be used provided one is not presented as a correction of the other;
- **Gol Gumbaj** is the spelling used in the standard account; the form Gol Gumbaz is also in common use, and both are recorded here without normalising either;
- the monument is recorded as **the mausoleum of Adil Shah**, and the school as developing under Adil Shah's patronage. The record does not distinguish between individual rulers of that dynasty, so no regnal number is attached here.

### Comparative note: four provincial schools, one specification

| Dimension | Bengal | Jaunpur | Malwa | Bijapur |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Dates recorded | 1203-1573 | 1394-1479 | 1405-1569 | 1490-1656 |
| Monumentality device | Curved sloping roof | Massive screen across the prayer-hall front | Coloured stone, stairs, arched pavilions | Three-arched facade and near-spherical dome |
| Minar | Not at issue in the record | Explicitly avoided | Explicitly avoided | Not recorded as avoided |
| Technical signature | Brick and basalt construction | Bold painted characters on the screens | Baulis, ventilation, batter | Iron clamps, strong mortar plaster, ceilings without apparent support |
| Named example | Adina and Qadam Rasul mosques | Atala Mosque | Rani Roopmati Pavilion, Jahaz Mahal, Ashrafi Mahal | Gol Gumbaj |

Read across the bottom row and the argument writes itself: four regions, four technical signatures, one liturgical specification.

### Examination use

Bijapur supplies the **strongest single dome description** in the topic, and a precise description of a dome profile is worth more than three additional monument names. It also supplies the **best evidence that provincial schools were technically ambitious rather than derivative**: iron clamps, strong mortar plaster and ceilings without apparent support are engineering claims, not decorative ones. In a **comparison demand** it anchors the Deccan corner of the four-school table. And the whispering-gallery box offers a rare cross-topic hook, provided the Victoria Memorial is correctly routed to Topic 05 and the Golghar location error is not reproduced.

### Revision notes

1. Bijapur School, 1490-1656 AD, under the patronage of Adil Shah; also called Bijapur style or the Deccan style.
2. Built mosques, tombs and palaces; unique in the use of a three-arched facade and bulbous dome.
3. The domes are almost spherical with a narrow neck - the phrase to reproduce.
4. Adil Shah introduced the use of cornices.
5. Special feature: ceilings treated without any apparent support.
6. Iron clamps and a strong plaster of mortar were used to give strength to the buildings; walls decorated by rich carvings.
7. Named example: Gol Gumbaj, the mausoleum of Adil Shah, in Bijapur or Vijayapura. The variant spelling Gol Gumbaz is in common use.
8. Bulbous versus hemispherical: the bulbous curve passes the half-sphere, so the widest point is above the springing and the shell is gathered into a neck at the drum.
9. Structural consequence: the overhanging mass concentrates load at the neck, which is why confident mortar work and iron clamping matter.
10. Visual consequence: a swelling mass on a slender waist reads as a single dominant object across a city - useful to competing regional powers.
11. The three-arched facade is the Deccan's device for a legible monumental front, parallel to Jaunpur's screen and Delhi's pishtaq.
12. The cornice throws off rainwater, casts a terminating horizontal shadow and gives the domes a base line; it recurs shallower in Sikh work and bowed in Rajput work.
13. Discipline on the ceiling: "without any apparent support" is what is recorded; no construction geometry is claimed here beyond iron clamps and strong mortar plaster.
14. Whispering gallery box: Gol Gumbaj, Victoria Memorial and the Golghar granary, printed as "Bandipore" in the source though the granary is at Bankipore, Patna. The Victoria Memorial belongs to Topic 05.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain precisely what distinguishes a bulbous dome from a hemispherical one, and why the Bijapur school's iron clamps and mortar plaster belong in the same answer.

**Model answer:** A hemispherical dome's curve terminates at the half-sphere, so its widest point coincides with the springing where it meets the drum or wall, and the shell's load descends in a straightforward compressive path. A bulbous dome carries the curve past the half-sphere, which places the widest point above the springing and forces the shell to be drawn back inward to meet its support - the narrow neck that the standard account describes when it calls the Bijapur domes almost spherical with a narrow neck. The consequence is that the overhanging mass tends to spread outward while the load is concentrated sharply at the constricted neck, so the profile is structurally more demanding than the hemisphere it replaces. That is exactly why the record of iron clamps and a strong plaster of mortar sits beside it: they are the means by which the school made its preferred silhouette buildable, and quoting the profile without the means describes an effect while omitting its cause.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating dome profile as pure aesthetics and clamps and mortar as incidental construction detail. In this topic the two are the same evidence viewed from different sides, and an answer that links ambition to means - a taller, more dramatic silhouette obtained through tying and strong bedding - demonstrates the structural literacy that distinguishes a high-scoring architectural answer from a descriptive one.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Examine the Bijapur or Deccan school as evidence that provincial Sultanate architecture was technically ambitious rather than derivative. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

The Bijapur school, active from 1490 to 1656 under Adil Shahi patronage, sets itself technical problems that imperial Delhi did not attempt.

Its dome is bulbous, almost spherical with a narrow neck, which carries the shell past the half-sphere and concentrates load at a constriction - a harder proposition than the hemisphere. Its response is explicit: iron clamps and a strong plaster of mortar to give the buildings strength.

Its interiors are recorded as treating ceilings without any apparent support, which is the arcuate system pressed to its limit, since the value of arch and dome is precisely the removal of intermediate props.

Its facades are organised by three arches and terminated by cornices, an order Adil Shah is credited with introducing, while the walls carry rich carving.

Gol Gumbaj embodies all of this. Derivative architecture does not invent a harder silhouette and then engineer a way to hold it.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the bulbous profile described exactly in the source's own terms 2 + the structural difficulty of the profile explained 2 + iron clamps and mortar plaster linked to that difficulty 2 + ceilings without apparent support read as the arcuate system at its limit 2 + the three-arched facade and the cornice introduction 1 + Gol Gumbaj named with its patron and city 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 11:** Four schools are on record with dates, devices and examples. But two regions that any complete treatment must mention are almost absent from the standard account - one of them represented by a photograph with no accompanying text. The next lesson handles Gujarat and Kashmir, and it is also a lesson in what to do when the sources thin out.

## Lesson 11 - Gujarat and Kashmir: two regions, and what to do when the evidence thins

Progress: 11/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Gujarat school's carved stone and jali tradition, the Kashmir timber mosque tradition, and the discipline required where the written record is thin

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Queried - the provincial schools section, and the Kashmir illustration that closes the Awadh passage without any accompanying text
CA search: "Not required - regional coverage and evidence-discipline lesson"
CA found: No current linkage required
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: how fully each region is documented, and what that permits

```text
  REGION       | HOW FULLY DOCUMENTED             | WHAT IS THEREFORE SAFE
  -------------|----------------------------------|--------------------------
  Bengal       | dates, material, roof form, two  | a complete school profile:
               | named mosques                    | device, driver, example
  -------------|----------------------------------|--------------------------
  Jaunpur      | dates, patrons, screen device,   | a complete school profile,
               | absence of minars, one mosque    | minus its material
  -------------|----------------------------------|--------------------------
  Malwa        | dates, two centres, five         | a complete school profile
               | adaptation devices, four named   | and a design programme
               | buildings                        |
  -------------|----------------------------------|--------------------------
  Bijapur      | dates, patron, facade, dome      | a complete school profile
               | profile, clamps, ceilings        | and an engineering claim
  -------------|----------------------------------|--------------------------
  GUJARAT      | not listed among the four named  | named monuments with dates,
               | provincial schools               | and one structural argument
  -------------|----------------------------------|--------------------------
  KASHMIR      | a caption and an illustration,   | two named monuments and a
               | with NO descriptive text;        | material driver only - no
               | Martand Sun Temple belongs to    | school dates, no feature
               | Topic 03's sun-temple list       | list, no classification
  -------------|----------------------------------|--------------------------

  --> DOCUMENTATION DENSITY SETS CLAIM STRENGTH. The two lower rows are
      taught with named monuments and one argument each, and are NOT
      written up as if they were documented to the depth of the four
      schools above.
```

That table is not decoration. It is an honest statement of how strongly each region can be claimed, and it belongs in the lesson because the habit it demonstrates - let the density of the evidence set the confidence of the claim - is itself examinable behaviour.

### 1. Why these two regions cannot simply be omitted

Three reasons.

First, the regional comparison this topic demands is explicit: **Delhi, Gujarat, Bengal, Malwa, Deccan, Rajasthan, Punjab and Awadh must be compared through material, climate and workshop, not flattened into one Indo-Islamic style.** A region named in that comparison cannot be silently dropped.

Second, both regions are conspicuous in the general study literature on this topic, so a learner who meets them elsewhere and finds them absent here will assume the treatment is incomplete rather than that the written record is thin.

Third, and most importantly, Gujarat is where the **jali** of Lesson 5 reaches its most celebrated expression, and Kashmir is where the entire structural argument of Lesson 2 is inverted, because its mosques are **timber-framed**. Both therefore test the topic's central claims rather than merely extending its list.

### 2. Gujarat: a carving industry meeting a new programme

**Beyond the four named schools.** Gujarat is not among the four provincial schools the standard account lists, so the following monuments are supplied here rather than drawn from it:

- the **Jami Masjid at Ahmedabad**, built in **1424** during the reign of **Sultan Ahmad Shah I**, a large congregational mosque combining regional carved-stone practice with the mosque specification, with many domes carried on slender pillars, carved screens and a large courtyard;
- the **Sidi Saiyyed mosque at Ahmedabad**, of **1572-73**, built by **Sidi Saiyyed**, famous for its pierced stone lattice windows and above all for the **"Tree of Life" jali**, which has become an emblem of the city;
- the **Jami Masjid at Champaner**, completed about **1513** under **Mahmud Begada**, combining the mosque plan with regional motifs, twin minarets, a pillared prayer hall, oriel windows and elaborate jali work;
- **Champaner-Pavagadh Archaeological Park** inscribed as a **UNESCO World Heritage Site in 2004**, and the **Historic City of Ahmadabad** inscribed in **2017**.

**What Gujarat adds analytically, and why it is worth the space.** The Gujarat school is the clearest case in the whole topic of a **trabeate craft tradition executing an arcuate programme**. The region possessed an exceptionally sophisticated stone-carving industry with deep experience in pillared halls, bracketed construction and pierced screens - the same industry that had produced western Indian temple architecture. When mosque building began, that industry did not retool; it applied its existing repertoire to the new specification. The result is mosque interiors dominated by carved pillars and beams rather than by arcades, with the jali developed to a degree unmatched elsewhere.

That makes Gujarat the school where the **method claim** of this topic - imported programme, indigenous craft - is visible in the structure of the building itself rather than only in its ornament.

**The discipline attached to this section.** These monuments support the argument above and the register notes. They are not used to derive any answer to an objective question, and they are not presented as part of the standard four-school classification, which does not include Gujarat.

### 3. Kashmir: a timber tradition, and an explicit gap

**The gap, stated first.** The standard account carries a heading and an illustration captioned **"Architecture in Kashmir"** at the end of its Awadh passage, with **no accompanying text**. The only Kashmiri entry with text anywhere nearby is the **Martand Sun Temple, Kashmir**, which appears in a list of sun temples and belongs to Topic 03's Hindu temple material, not here. Kashmir is nowhere treated as a provincial school. So the written record for this topic supplies a caption and nothing else.

**What can nevertheless be taught, carefully:**

- the **Jama Masjid at Srinagar**, first built about **1400** under **Sultan Sikandar** and extended subsequently, built as a **timber structure carried on wooden deodar columns**, with a large square courtyard and a high **pyramidal roof** - a form distinct from the masonry mosques of the plains;
- the **Khanqah-e-Moula, also called the Shah Hamadan mosque**, at Srinagar, originally of the **late fourteenth century** and rebuilt several times after fires, an **entirely wooden structure** with a **multi-tiered sloping roof** and carved and painted interiors, associated with the Sufi figure **Mir Sayyid Ali Hamadani**;
- the material driver in both cases being the **abundance of deodar timber** in the Himalayan region.

**What Kashmir adds analytically.** It inverts the topic's structural premise. Lessons 2 and 3 argued that the decisive imported technology was arcuate - arch, vault, dome, mortar. Kashmir's mosques are **trabeate and timber-framed**, with a pyramidal or tiered roof instead of a dome, and they satisfy the same liturgical specification perfectly well. That is the sharpest available demonstration that the specification is about **orientation, congregation and shelter**, not about a particular structural system.

It also gives the topic its widest material spread. Set the four regions side by side - Bengal's brick, Malwa's coloured stone, Bijapur's clamped masonry, Kashmir's deodar - and the claim that material and climate govern form has been demonstrated across the whole subcontinent rather than asserted.

**The discipline attached to this section.** Because the written record supplies only a caption, no "Kashmir school" with a full feature list is built here, no school dates are assigned, and the region is not attached to the imperial-provincial classification that the standard account applies to Bengal, Jaunpur, Malwa and Bijapur. Two named monuments, a material driver and one analytical point are all that is claimed.

### 4. The method this lesson is really teaching

When the written record thins, there are three bad responses and one good one.

- **Bad: silence.** Omitting a region the topic itself names for comparison leaves a gap that the examiner may well probe.
- **Bad: inflation.** Writing Gujarat and Kashmir up at the same depth as Bengal or Bijapur, using half-remembered material, imports exactly the kind of unsourced claim this topic repeatedly punishes.
- **Bad: false precision.** Attaching dates, feature lists and school boundaries to regions for which the record supplies none.
- **Good: bounded supplementation.** State what the record holds. Add a small, checked set of named monuments. Use them for argument, not for deriving answers to keyed questions. And say openly where the gap remains.

That is not merely a housekeeping habit. In an answer it reads as judgement, and judgement is what separates a 60 per cent script from an 80 per cent one.

### Comparative note: the widest material spread in the topic

| Region | Structural material | Roof or skyline | What it proves |
|---|---|---|---|
| Bengal | Brick, with imported black basalt | Curved bangaldar roof | Delta geology and rainfall drive form |
| Malwa | Different coloured stones and marbles | Lightly arched pavilions | Heat and water storage drive form |
| Bijapur | Clamped and mortared masonry | Bulbous dome, almost spherical with a narrow neck | Regional ambition drives structural risk |
| Gujarat | Finely carved local stone, pillared and bracketed | Multiple domes on slender pillars | A trabeate craft industry can execute an arcuate programme |
| Kashmir | Deodar timber | Pyramidal or multi-tiered sloping roof | The mosque specification does not require the arcuate system at all |

The last row is the strongest single line available for a synthesis answer's qualification, and it costs one clause.

### Examination use

This lesson has two distinct uses. Substantively, **Gujarat** is the best evidence for the method claim - indigenous craft executing an imported programme - and **Kashmir** is the best evidence that the programme is separable from the technology. Methodologically, the lesson supplies a model for the **evidence-discipline sentence** that any strong answer can carry: name what is well attested, mark what is added, and state where a gap remains. In a 20-mark answer that sentence is worth more than an additional monument, because it demonstrates control over the material rather than volume of it.

### Revision notes

1. Gujarat is one of the regions this topic requires to be compared, but it is not one of the four provincial schools named in the standard account.
2. Kashmir appears in the standard account only as a heading and an illustration captioned "Architecture in Kashmir", with no accompanying text; Martand Sun Temple appears separately in a sun-temple list and belongs to Topic 03.
3. The Gujarat and Kashmir facts below are supplied beyond the standard four-school account, and are used for argument rather than for deriving keyed answers.
4. Gujarat: Jami Masjid, Ahmedabad, 1424, under Sultan Ahmad Shah I - many domes on slender pillars, carved screens, large courtyard.
5. Gujarat: Sidi Saiyyed mosque, Ahmedabad, 1572-73, built by Sidi Saiyyed - pierced stone lattice windows, the celebrated "Tree of Life" jali.
6. Gujarat: Jami Masjid, Champaner, about 1513, under Mahmud Begada - twin minarets, pillared prayer hall, oriel windows, elaborate jali.
7. Gujarat heritage anchors: Champaner-Pavagadh Archaeological Park inscribed 2004; Historic City of Ahmadabad inscribed 2017. Counts and policy route to Topic 14.
8. Gujarat's analytical value: a sophisticated trabeate stone-carving industry executing an arcuate programme, producing pillared mosque interiors and the finest jali work in the tradition.
9. Kashmir: Jama Masjid, Srinagar, first built about 1400 under Sultan Sikandar, timber on deodar columns, large square courtyard, high pyramidal roof.
10. Kashmir: Khanqah-e-Moula or Shah Hamadan mosque, Srinagar, late fourteenth century, rebuilt after fires, entirely wooden, multi-tiered sloping roof, associated with Mir Sayyid Ali Hamadani.
11. Kashmir's driver: abundant deodar timber in the Himalayan region.
12. Kashmir's analytical value: trabeate timber mosques with pyramidal roofs satisfy the same liturgical specification without the arcuate system, proving the programme is separable from the technology.
13. No school dates, feature lists or imperial-provincial classification are asserted for Kashmir, because the record supplies none.
14. The method: state what the record holds, add only checked supplementation, use it for argument rather than for deriving keyed answers, and say where the gap remains.

### Concept check

**Question:** Kashmir's medieval mosques are timber-framed with pyramidal roofs and no domes. Does this weaken the claim that arcuate construction was the decisive technological import of the period? Give a reasoned answer.

**Model answer:** It does not weaken the claim, but it sharpens what the claim can mean. The arcuate system was decisive for the *monumental* building programme at imperial and regional courts, because column-free wide prayer halls, great gateways and freestanding domed tombs depend on spanning with stone in compression. Kashmir shows that this was a decisive technology for a particular ambition, not a requirement of the religious programme itself: a mosque needs orientation towards the qibla, a hall that can hold rows, ablution facilities and shelter, and all four are perfectly obtainable in timber. The Srinagar Jama Masjid, built about 1400 on deodar columns with a high pyramidal roof, and the wholly wooden Khanqah-e-Moula, meet the specification without a single arch of consequence. The correct formulation is therefore that arcuate construction was decisive wherever monumental span and a dominating silhouette were wanted, and dispensable where local material and climate offered a different answer.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that a region's absence from a textbook's list of provincial schools means the region had no significant tradition. The four named schools are the ones that account selects; Gujarat's mosque architecture and Kashmir's timber mosques exist independently of that selection. The disciplined response is to supplement in a bounded way rather than either to pretend the gap is not there or to fill it with unsourced recollection.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Material and climate, more than patronage, explain the diversity of medieval Indian mosque architecture." Examine. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

Material and climate explain most of the visible diversity. Bengal, lacking building stone in its delta but rich in brick clay and subject to heavy rain, built in brick with imported black basalt and roofed its mosques with the curved bangaldar form taken from the hut. Malwa, hot and dry, used large ventilating windows, lightly arched pavilions and baulis for water storage. Kashmir, rich in deodar, built timber mosques on wooden columns under high pyramidal roofs, dispensing with the dome altogether. Gujarat's superb stone-carving industry produced pillared, screened interiors and the finest jali work in the tradition.

Patronage nonetheless retains real explanatory force. Jaunpur's Sharqi rulers and the Malwa school both refused minars, which no climate requires, and Bijapur's Adil Shahi patrons chose a technically riskier dome profile for dominance.

Material and climate set the possibilities; patronage chose among them, and sometimes chose against the obvious.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = four regions each tied to a specific material or climatic driver 4 + the Kashmir case used to show the dome is not required 1 + the counterweight that minar refusal and dome ambition are patronage choices 2 + an explicit both-and verdict rather than a false either-or 2 + a stated caution where the record is thin 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 12:** The Sultanate half of the topic is complete - imperial phases, provincial schools, and the two regions the record leaves thin. Now a new dynasty arrives with a Central Asian background, an inherited Timurid garden tradition, and almost no time to build. What the first three Mughal generations actually achieved is more surprising, and more consequential, than their short reigns suggest.
## Lesson 12 - Mughal beginnings: Babur, Humayun and the Sur interregnum

### Visual: three short reigns, and what each actually leaves behind

