```text
  Rohilkhand (1526)             "could not finish it"    Delhi
  Kabuli Bagh Mosque,                 |                  Rohtas Fort (Pakistan)
  Panipat, 1527, marking        LEFT BEHIND:             Sher Shah Suri Masjid,
  victory over Ibrahim          Persian style becomes    Patna, "Afghan style"
  Lodhi at Panipat, 1526        prominent in the              |
        |                       architecture of the      AND:
  EARLIER, AT KABUL:            period                   Sadak-e-Azam, the
  Bagh-e Babur, 1504,                 |                  rebuilt Mauryan route,
  "one of the earliest          AFTER HIS DEATH:         later the Grand Trunk
  surviving Mughal              HUMAYUN'S TOMB,          Road, with sarais and
  gardens in the world"         commissioned by BEGA     trees
        |                       BEGUM in 1558;                 |
        v                       UNESCO WHS 1993          TOMB: Sasaram, his
  THE GARDEN ARRIVES                  |                  birthplace; RED
  BEFORE THE BUILDINGS                v                  SANDSTONE; SITUATED
                                THE GARDEN TOMB          INSIDE A LAKE
                                ARRIVES AT                     |
                                MONUMENTAL SCALE               v
                                                         "continued the
                                                          traditions of the
                                                          Delhi Sultanate"

  THE LESSON'S ARGUMENT: the dynasty's most consequential early contribution
  is not a building style but a LANDSCAPE IDEA (the garden) and a TOMB TYPE
  (the garden tomb), both of which outlive the short reigns that produced them
```

Read the diagram across and the standard summary - "the first Mughals built little" - survives, but the interesting version appears beneath it: what they established was not a manner of decorating walls but two durable *types*, the formal garden and the garden tomb.

### 1. Babur: a garden before a style

The standard account is candid. Babur **undertook the construction of mosques in Panipat and Rohilkhand, both constructed in 1526 AD**, but **his reign was too short-lived to inspire any new style or technique**. It names the **Kabuli Bagh Mosque**, which **Babur constructed in 1527 in Panipat, in present-day Haryana, to mark his victory over Sultan Ibrahim Lodhi at the Battle of Panipat in 1526**. And it records that **Babur built Bagh-e Babur in Kabul in 1504 after his conquest of Kabul in 1504**, describing it as **one of the earliest surviving Mughal gardens in the world**.

**An internal inconsistency in the standard account.** The same passage says the mosques at Panipat and Rohilkhand were **both constructed in 1526 AD** and then dates the Kabuli Bagh Mosque at Panipat to **1527**. Both dates are recorded here as printed; the commemorative logic - a mosque marking the 1526 victory, built in or shortly after 1526 - is stated, and neither date is silently selected.

**What Babur's entry is actually worth.** Three things.

- **The commemorative mosque.** A mosque built to mark a battlefield victory is architecture used as a political record, at the very start of the dynasty. That is the Lesson 1 fourth question - message - appearing immediately.
- **The garden priority.** Bagh-e Babur predates the Indian conquest by twenty-two years. The garden tradition the Mughals bring to India is therefore not something they developed here; it arrives fully formed. This is the strongest single piece of evidence for the "transferred vocabulary" half of the synthesis argument, because a garden layout is a portable idea in a way that a masonry technique is not.
- **The honest negative.** The source's own statement that the reign was too short to inspire a new style is worth quoting, because it sets up the correct reading of the whole early phase: continuity first, distinctiveness later.

### 2. Humayun: an unfinished city and a posthumous monument

The standard account records that **Humayun's reign was marked by constant power struggle against Sher Shah Suri**, so **he could not focus much on art and architecture**. He **led the foundation of a city named Dinpanah, but could not finish it**. And - the sentence that matters - **Persian style became prominent in the architecture of this period**.

Then: **Humayun's Tomb: the tomb was commissioned by Bega Begum in 1558, after the death of Humayun. UNESCO declared the tomb as the World Heritage Site in 1993.**

**Four points worth drawing out.**

- **The patron is a woman, and the building is posthumous.** Humayun did not build his own tomb; **Bega Begum commissioned it in 1558**, after his death. This is the first of two major Mughal monuments on record commissioned by women - the second is Itmad-ud-Daulah's tomb, commissioned by Nur Jahan, in Lesson 14. That is a patronage pattern worth naming explicitly in an answer, because it complicates the standard emperor-centred narrative.
- **The Persian style statement is the dynasty's stylistic inheritance, stated plainly.** It is the counterpart to Babur's garden: a court aesthetic arriving from outside.
- **The tomb is where the garden and the tomb types fuse.** Lesson 4 defined the garden tomb as a centralised mausoleum placed at the crossing of a charbagh's axes. Humayun's tomb is the monumental Indian arrival of that type.
- **The double dome develops here.** As Lessons 3 and 7 established, the mature double dome belongs to the **Lodi-Humayun transition**, with **Sikandar Lodi's tomb of 1517 as the early Indian case** and **Humayun's tomb developing the device at monumental scale**. So the single most consequential formal device of the period is completed on this building.

**The chronological oddity that makes this examinable.** Humayun could not build during his reign, and his most important architectural legacy postdates his death by at least four years and was paid for by his widow. Any answer that writes "under Humayun, Mughal architecture developed the double dome and the garden tomb" has compressed a genuinely interesting sequence into a false one. The accurate version - a reign with almost no building, followed by a posthumous monument that establishes the dynasty's tomb type - is both truer and more interesting.

### 3. Sher Shah and the Sur interregnum: the recorded bridge

The standard account gives Sher Shah a substantial entry, and it is more useful than its length suggests. **During his brief reign, Sher Shah built few monuments.** Recorded works:

- the **Qila-i-Kuhna, the Mosque of the Old Fort, in Delhi**;
- the **Rohtas Fort in Pakistan**;
- the **Sher Shah Suri Masjid in Patna**, built **in Afghan style to mark his reign**.

And the interpretive sentence: **his period is a transition from Lodi style to Mughal style of architecture.**

Two further entries belong here:

- the infrastructure work - **the reconstruction and extension of an old Mauryan route, renamed Sadak-e-Azam, the Great Road, which was later called the Grand Trunk Road**, with **adequate presence of sarais and trees for travellers**;
- the tomb - **Sher Shah Suri's tomb was built at his birthplace Sasaram**, **made up of red sandstone** and **situated inside a lake**.

Closing: **the constructions under Sher Shah continued the traditions of the Delhi Sultanate period.**

**Why the interregnum earns its lesson space.**

- **It is the recorded bridge.** The record explicitly calls this period a transition from Lodi to Mughal style. A candidate who can name the bridge has a chronology; one who jumps from Lodi tombs to Akbar's sandstone has a gap.
- **Sasaram is a type-defining monument.** A red sandstone tomb set inside a lake is a variant of the garden tomb where water, rather than planting, provides the setting. Put it beside Humayun's tomb of the same decade and you have two different answers to the same design question - how to isolate and elevate a mausoleum within a designed landscape.
- **The road is architecture at the largest scale.** Sarais - travellers' rest houses - along a rebuilt trunk route constitute a building programme distributed across a subcontinent. Lesson 19 uses this when it argues that this tradition builds at four scales, of which the largest is infrastructural and urban. The route's political and economic history belongs to Medieval Indian History; the **sarai as a building type and the road as a designed system** belong here.
- **It supplies one reproduced objective route.** A reproduced objective item asks which building cannot be attributed to Shah Jahan's reign, offering the Moti Masjid, the Qila-i-Kuhna mosque, the Jama Masjid and the Rang Mahal; the printed key selects the Qila-i-Kuhna, which is Sher Shah's. That item is set out with its status in the examination section.

### 4. What the first three generations actually establish

Stated as a short argument, because this is the paragraph an answer needs:

1. **A portable landscape idea** - the formal garden, present at Kabul in 1504, long before any Indian building.
2. **A court aesthetic** - Persian style becoming prominent under Humayun.
3. **A monumental type** - the garden tomb, arriving at scale with Humayun's tomb, commissioned by Bega Begum in 1558.
4. **A completed device** - the double dome, appearing in India under the Lodis and developed monumentally on that same tomb.
5. **A continuity** - Sur-period construction continuing Delhi Sultanate traditions, explicitly described as the transition from Lodi to Mughal style.

Notice what is *not* on the list: a distinctive material, a distinctive ornament system, or a distinctive scale. Those arrive with Akbar, which is exactly why the standard account says the golden period of Mughal art and architecture started **after Akbar ascended the throne of Delhi in 1556**.

### Comparative note: two tombs of the same generation

| Dimension | Sher Shah Suri's tomb, Sasaram | Humayun's tomb, Delhi |
|---|---|---|
| Patron | Built at his birthplace; the record does not name the commissioning patron | Commissioned by Bega Begum in 1558, after Humayun's death |
| Material recorded | Red sandstone | Not specified on record |
| Setting | Situated inside a lake | The monumental garden-tomb setting |
| Stylistic position | Continues Delhi Sultanate traditions; the Lodi-to-Mughal transition | Develops the double dome at monumental scale; Persian style prominent |
| Heritage status recorded | None recorded | UNESCO World Heritage Site, 1993 |

The table's fourth row is the comparison worth writing: two near-contemporary tombs, one closing the Sultanate tradition and one opening the Mughal one.

### Examination use

This lesson protects against the single commonest compression in Mughal-development answers - jumping from the Lodis to Akbar. Three usable moves. First, name **Bagh-e Babur of 1504** to show the garden preceded the conquest, which is precise evidence for the transferred-vocabulary half of a synthesis argument. Second, state **Humayun's tomb accurately**: commissioned by Bega Begum in 1558 after his death, inscribed in 1993, and the monument on which the double dome reaches monumental scale - a sentence that is correct, dense and unusual. Third, use **Sher Shah** as the named bridge, with Sasaram as a tomb-in-a-lake beside the garden tomb, and the Qila-i-Kuhna as the answer to the reproduced objective item on Shah Jahan attributions.

### Revision notes

1. Babur: mosques at Panipat and Rohilkhand, both recorded as constructed in 1526 AD; the reign was too short-lived to inspire any new style or technique.
2. Kabuli Bagh Mosque: constructed in 1527 at Panipat, present-day Haryana, marking the victory over Sultan Ibrahim Lodhi at the Battle of Panipat in 1526. The 1526 and 1527 dates in the same passage are an internal inconsistency, recorded and not resolved.
3. Bagh-e Babur, Kabul, built in 1504 after Babur's conquest of Kabul in the same year; one of the earliest surviving Mughal gardens in the world. The garden precedes the Indian conquest by twenty-two years.
4. Humayun: constant power struggle against Sher Shah Suri; could not focus on art and architecture; founded the city of Dinpanah but could not finish it.
5. Persian style became prominent in the architecture of Humayun's period.
6. Humayun's Tomb: commissioned by Bega Begum in 1558, after Humayun's death; UNESCO World Heritage Site 1993.
7. Two major monuments on record are commissioned by women - Humayun's tomb by Bega Begum and Itmad-ud-Daulah's tomb by Nur Jahan.
8. Placement: the mature double dome belongs to the Lodi-Humayun transition, with Sikandar Lodi's tomb of 1517 as the early Indian case and Humayun's tomb developing it at monumental scale.
9. Sher Shah: built few monuments - Qila-i-Kuhna, the Mosque of the Old Fort, Delhi; Rohtas Fort in Pakistan; Sher Shah Suri Masjid in Patna, in Afghan style, to mark his reign.
10. His period is a transition from Lodi style to Mughal style of architecture; constructions under him continued the traditions of the Delhi Sultanate period.
11. Sher Shah rebuilt and extended an old Mauryan route as the Sadak-e-Azam or Great Road, later the Grand Trunk Road, with sarais and trees for travellers.
12. Sher Shah Suri's tomb: at his birthplace Sasaram; red sandstone; situated inside a lake.
13. The first three generations establish a garden idea, a court aesthetic, a monumental tomb type, a completed structural device and a continuity - but not yet a distinctive material or ornament system.
14. The standard account dates the start of the golden period of Mughal art and architecture to Akbar's accession to the throne of Delhi in 1556.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is it inaccurate to say that "Mughal architecture developed the garden tomb and the double dome under Humayun", even though Humayun's tomb is the monument where both appear at scale?

**Model answer:** Because it attributes to a reign what belongs partly to an inheritance and partly to a posthumous commission. The garden element was not developed in India at all: Bagh-e Babur was built at Kabul in 1504, twenty-two years before the Indian conquest, and is recorded as one of the earliest surviving Mughal gardens, so the layout arrived fully formed. The double dome was introduced in India in the Lodi period, with Sikandar Lodi's tomb of 1517 as the early case; Humayun's tomb develops it at monumental scale rather than inventing it. And the tomb itself was commissioned by Bega Begum in 1558, after Humayun's death, during a reign that the record describes as too preoccupied with the struggle against Sher Shah Suri for architectural patronage. The accurate statement is that Humayun's tomb is where an inherited garden idea, a Lodi structural device and a centralised tomb type were combined at monumental scale under a posthumous, female patron.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the ruler's name as the unit of architectural analysis. Reigns are a convenient index, but buildings have commissioners, completion dates and inherited components that frequently cross reign boundaries. The same error produces "Aibak built the Qutb Minar" in Lesson 6 and "Shah Jahan's architect designed the Taj" in Lesson 15; in each case the correction is the same - separate initiation, execution, completion and inheritance.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"The first three Mughal generations contributed types rather than a style." Examine. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

The claim is well supported. Babur's reign is recorded as too short to inspire any new style or technique; his Indian works are mosques at Panipat and Rohilkhand, with the Kabuli Bagh Mosque commemorating the victory of 1526. Humayun, absorbed by the struggle against Sher Shah Suri, left Dinpanah unfinished. Sher Shah built few monuments, and his constructions are described as continuing Delhi Sultanate traditions, his period forming the transition from Lodi to Mughal style.

Yet the types established are decisive. The formal garden arrived complete, Bagh-e Babur having been built at Kabul in 1504. The garden tomb reached monumental scale at Humayun's tomb, commissioned by Bega Begum in 1558, where the double dome introduced under the Lodis was developed monumentally. Sasaram offered a rival setting, a red sandstone tomb inside a lake.

A distinctive material and ornament system waited for Akbar.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the three recorded negatives on stylistic contribution 3 + the garden as a pre-conquest inheritance with Bagh-e Babur dated 2 + Humayun's tomb stated with its posthumous female patron and date 2 + the double dome placed at the Lodi-Humayun transition 1 + Sasaram as the alternative tomb setting 1 + the closing point that material and ornament arrive with Akbar 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 13:** The types are in place; the manner is not. With Akbar comes the first unmistakable Mughal signature - a single dominant material, a distinctive arch profile, construction at the scale of forts and a whole capital city, and a building programme in which architecture is used to stage policy. This is the largest single block of evidence in the topic.

### Visual: Fatehpur Sikri as a programme, not a list

The diagram groups the complex by **function**, and that regrouping is itself the answer to most questions about Fatehpur Sikri. The site is usually memorised as an unordered list of eight names. Sorted into religious, governmental, residential and deliberative, it becomes what it actually is - a capital designed so that each activity of sovereignty has a purpose-built stage.

### 1. Akbar's signature: one material and one arch

The standard account: **the principal feature of the constructions during Akbar's reign was the use of red sandstone**, and **he also introduced the use of the "Tudor arch", the four-centred arch**.

**Why red sandstone is a signature and not a detail.** A single dominant material across forts, palaces, gateways and mosques produces visual unity across an empire's building programme, and it is a logistical statement as well as an aesthetic one - it implies quarries, transport, and the administrative capacity to supply distant sites from them. This is the beginning of the **material-as-marker** argument that Lesson 14 completes.

**What a four-centred arch is.** An arch struck from four centres rather than two: the lower portions of the curve rise steeply from the springing on tight radii, and the upper portions run on much longer radii to meet in a flattened point at the crown. The profile is therefore **wide, low and gently pointed** rather than steeply pointed. Architecturally it suits broad openings in heavy walls, and it gives Akbari fabric its characteristic breadth. The name "Tudor arch" is borrowed from English usage for the same geometry, and the standard account uses it directly.

**Why the profile matters for reading buildings.** Arch profile, like dome profile in Lesson 3, is a dating and attribution cue that requires no inscription. A wide four-centred opening in red sandstone is an Akbari signature in the way that a bulbous dome on a narrow neck is a Deccan one.

### 2. Agra Fort: one monument, two reigns

The standard account records Agra Fort as **one of the first constructions which started during the reign of Akbar**, with the fort **built between 1565 and 1573**. But it immediately qualifies: **most of the structures present inside the fort were constructed during the reign of Shah Jahan**. The buildings it lists inside are attributed accordingly - **Moti Masjid, by Shah Jahan**; **Diwan-i-aam, the Hall of Public Audience, by Shah Jahan**; **Diwan-i-khas, the Hall of Private Audience, by Shah Jahan**; **Jahangiri Mahal**; and **Sheesh Mahal, the Turkish Bath**. The gardens inside the fort are **built using the Charbagh style**. The account also records that during Akbar's reign **more than 5000 women stayed in his harem inside this fort**.

**The examinable point is the attribution split.** Agra Fort is the second great layered monument of this topic, after the Qutb Minar. Writing "Akbar built Agra Fort with its Diwan-i-Aam and Diwan-i-Khas" collapses two reigns, exactly as "Aibak built the Qutb Minar" collapses three. The correct sentence names the enclosure as Akbar's, dated 1565-1573, and the principal interior structures as Shah Jahan's.

**A caution on the harem figure.** The number given for the harem is reported here as the standard account states it. It is a court-scale claim rather than an architectural measurement, it belongs to the social history of the Mughal court, and no architectural argument is built on it.

### 3. Fatehpur Sikri: the complete record

**Foundation and duration.** Located in **Agra District, Uttar Pradesh**; **founded in 1571 as the new capital after Agra**, by Akbar, **following his victorious Gujarat campaign**; **capital for a short period from 1571 to 1585**, after which the capital moved **first to Lahore, later back to Agra**.

**Buland Darwaza.** The **world's highest gateway**, **built by Akbar to commemorate his victory over Gujarat**, forming **the grand entrance of the Jama Masjid** there. **Primarily constructed using red and buff sandstone**, **decorated with geometric designs and Persian inscriptions**, including the saying **"The world is but a bridge, pass over it, but build no houses upon it."**

This is the purest example in the topic of Lesson 4's **pishtaq** taken to its limit: a rectangular frame so tall that it dwarfs the building behind it. And the inscription is the clearest case of Lesson 5's calligraphy carrying explicit content - a gateway proclaiming the transience of the world is a deliberate juxtaposition of permanence in stone with a text about impermanence.

**Tomb of Sheikh Salim Chishti.** Built by Akbar **in honour of the Sufi saint whose blessings ensured the birth of Akbar's heir, Salim, who later became Jahangir**. **Constructed entirely in white marble**, with **exquisite jaali screens with complex geometric patterns, black marble inscriptions, and painted floral motifs**; **the cenotaph lies in the central chamber**, making it a place of pilgrimage and prayer.

This monument carries one of the sharpest analytical points in the whole topic. **In an overwhelmingly red sandstone capital, the one white marble building is a saint's tomb.** Marble is not yet the imperial material - that comes under Jahangir, in Lesson 14 - but it is already the *sacred* material. A candidate who notices that Akbar reserved the more precious stone for the shrine rather than for his own halls has read the site rather than listed it.

**Jama Masjid.** Described in the record as **"the glory of Fatehpur."**

**Jodha Bai's Palace**, also **Mariam-uz-Zamani's Palace**: a **large royal residence, blended with Hindu motifs along with Mughal design**, and **noted for blue-glazed tile roofs**. Two things here. The Hindu motifs are direct architectural evidence for the anti-essentialism argument, inside an imperial palace. The blue-glazed tile roofs are a distinct technique - glazed ceramic tilework - not otherwise recorded for this topic, and worth naming for that reason.

**Diwan-i-Aam**: an **extensive courtyard, enclosed by cloisters of one hundred and eleven bays**, where **Akbar heard petitions and dispensed justice**. The exact bay count is a usable precise fact.

**Diwan-i-Khas**, the **Jewel House**: **a unique building in the entire range of Indo-Islamic architecture**. **Raised on a square plan**, **outwardly giving the appearance of a double-storeyed structure**, it **consists of a single vaulted chamber open from floor to roof, with an opening on each of the four sides**. This is the disjunction between exterior reading and interior reality introduced in Lesson 4 - the same logic as the double dome, applied to a whole building.

**Panch Mahal**: a **five-storeyed structure that contains 176 columns**. Note what the column count implies: this is a **trabeate** building, a stack of open pillared storeys, at the heart of the most celebrated Mughal capital. It is the single most economical rebuttal available to the claim that arcuate construction replaced trabeate.

**Ibadat Khana**: **constructed by Akbar in 1575**, as a place where **Akbar held religious and spiritual discussions and debates with scholars of various religions**. It **became a forum for interfaith dialogue and Akbar's experiment with Sulh-i-Kul**, the religious policy of universal tolerance. This **led Akbar to conclude that no single faith held the monopoly of truth and inspired him to formulate Din-i-Ilahi, in 1582**, a **syncretic spiritual order blending elements from various religions, though it never had mass acceptance**.

**Pachisi Court**: **a courtyard designed like a giant board**. The record's own sentence adds that this is where **Akbar is believed to have played chess**; since the court is named for pachisi, the description is reported here as printed and the inconsistency is noted rather than reproduced as a settled fact.

**Hiran Minar**: a **red sandstone memorial tower built by Akbar in the memory of his favourite elephant, Hiran**, and **decorated with elephant-tusk-like projections**, reflecting both Mughal hunting culture and Akbar's personal sentiments. Note that a **minar** here is not a mosque tower at all but a free-standing commemorative tower - a useful reminder that the term names a form, not a function.

**Afterlife and status.** **After Akbar, no Mughal ruler made any significant architectural contribution in the Fatehpur Sikri complex.** **Fatehpur Sikri was inscribed as a UNESCO World Heritage Site in 1986**, and UNESCO describes it as an **"extraordinary ensemble of Mughal architecture"**, combining **Persian-Islamic elements with indigenous Indian styles**.

That last clause is worth keeping verbatim in an answer: the heritage citation itself states the synthesis claim, which means a candidate arguing for synthesis at Fatehpur Sikri is not offering a private interpretation.

### 4. Allahabad Fort, and the third scale

**Allahabad Fort was built by Akbar in 1583 near the confluence of the Yamuna with the Ganges. The famous Allahabad Pillar of Ashoka is presently located inside Allahabad Fort premises.**

Two points. The **confluence siting** is a deliberate choice of a place already charged with meaning, which is the Lesson 19 argument about architecture and landscape. And the **Ashokan pillar inside the fort** is a second, quieter instance of the reuse theme of Lesson 6 - here not demolition but enclosure, an earlier monument absorbed into a later one. The word to use is *relocation and enclosure*, not spolia, since no reuse of material is recorded.

### 5. The boundary this lesson must hold

The Ibadat Khana passage runs directly into *Sulh-i-Kul* and *Din-i-Ilahi*, and it is very easy to keep writing. The line is drawn explicitly: **Medieval History owns the full Delhi Sultanate and Mughal political and military narrative and the detailed doctrinal content of Akbar's religious policy; Medieval History's Akbar topic owns the religious policy; Art and Culture Topic 13 owns only its religious-cultural-synthesis lens; this topic owns the architectural setting and form only.**

The analytical reason follows: **the physical setting - the Ibadat Khana - and the doctrinal outcome - Din-i-Ilahi's limited following - must be kept analytically distinct.**

So the disciplined position is:

- **Owned here:** that a hall was **constructed in 1575** at Fatehpur Sikri for debates with scholars of various religions, that it functioned as a forum for interfaith dialogue, and that it is the **built setting** in which the policy was developed. That the built setting outlived the policy is itself an architectural observation, and a good one.
- **Not owned here:** the content, mechanics, reception or historiography of *Sulh-i-Kul* and *Din-i-Ilahi* beyond the recorded fact that the latter, formulated in **1582**, **never had mass acceptance**.

When the 2025 Mains question on Akbar's religious syncretism is discussed in the examination section, this is the boundary that governs how this topic contributes to it.

### Comparative note: the two audience halls, and what a plan reveals

| Dimension | Diwan-i-Aam, Fatehpur Sikri | Diwan-i-Khas, Fatehpur Sikri |
|---|---|---|
| Recorded form | Extensive courtyard enclosed by cloisters of 111 bays | Square plan; outwardly double-storeyed; internally one vaulted chamber open floor to roof, with an opening on each of four sides |
| Implied audience | Large, public, petitioning | Small, selected, admitted through four controlled approaches |
| Spatial logic | Perimeter cloisters contain and order a crowd | A single volume with four symmetrical entries and no internal division |
| What the form says | Accessibility, staged and bounded | Exclusivity, with an exterior that misreports the interior |

Set side by side, the two buildings demonstrate that Mughal court architecture encoded **degrees of access** in plan. That is a stronger point than either building alone.

### Examination use

This lesson is the largest evidence block in the topic and serves four demand families. For a **development demand**, Akbar supplies the first distinctive material and arch profile. For an **ideology demand**, the Buland Darwaza, the functional zoning of Fatehpur Sikri and the two audience halls give architecture-as-statement with exact detail. For a **synthesis demand**, three items are decisive and all are inside imperial fabric - the Hindu motifs and blue-glazed tile roofs of Jodha Bai's Palace, the 176-column trabeate Panch Mahal, and the UNESCO citation's own phrase about Persian-Islamic elements combined with indigenous Indian styles. For the **2025 Akbar syncretism demand**, this topic supplies the Ibadat Khana of 1575 as the architectural setting and stops there.

### Revision notes

1. Akbar's principal material: red sandstone. He introduced the "Tudor arch", the four-centred arch.
2. A four-centred arch rises steeply on tight radii and flattens to a gentle point on long radii - wide, low, broad; the Akbari signature profile.
3. Agra Fort: begun in Akbar's reign, built between 1565 and 1573; most interior structures are Shah Jahan's - Moti Masjid, Diwan-i-aam and Diwan-i-khas by Shah Jahan; Jahangiri Mahal and Sheesh Mahal, the Turkish Bath, also named; gardens in Charbagh style.
4. Agra Fort is the topic's second layered monument after the Qutb Minar; never attribute the interior structures to Akbar.
5. Fatehpur Sikri: Agra District, Uttar Pradesh; founded 1571 as the new capital after Agra, following the victorious Gujarat campaign; capital 1571-1585; then Lahore, later back to Agra; UNESCO World Heritage Site 1986.
6. Buland Darwaza: world's highest gateway; commemorates the victory over Gujarat; grand entrance to the Jama Masjid; red and buff sandstone; geometric designs and Persian inscriptions, including "The world is but a bridge, pass over it, but build no houses upon it."
7. Tomb of Sheikh Salim Chishti: built by Akbar in honour of the Sufi saint whose blessings ensured the birth of Salim, later Jahangir; entirely white marble; exquisite jaali screens with complex geometric patterns; black marble inscriptions; painted floral motifs; cenotaph in the central chamber.
8. The analytical point: in a red sandstone capital, the one white marble building is a saint's tomb - marble is the sacred material before it becomes the imperial one.
9. Jama Masjid at Fatehpur Sikri, described as "the glory of Fatehpur".
10. Jodha Bai's or Mariam-uz-Zamani's Palace: large royal residence; Hindu motifs blended with Mughal design; blue-glazed tile roofs.
11. Diwan-i-Aam: extensive courtyard enclosed by cloisters of one hundred and eleven bays; Akbar heard petitions and dispensed justice there.
12. Diwan-i-Khas, the Jewel House: unique in the entire range of Indo-Islamic architecture; square plan; outwardly double-storeyed; internally a single vaulted chamber open from floor to roof with an opening on each of the four sides.
13. Panch Mahal: five storeys, 176 columns - a trabeate building at the heart of the capital, and the shortest rebuttal to the replacement thesis.
14. Ibadat Khana: constructed 1575; debates with scholars of various religions; forum for interfaith dialogue and for Sulh-i-Kul; led to Din-i-Ilahi in 1582, which never had mass acceptance.
15. Boundary: this topic owns the Ibadat Khana as a built setting. Doctrinal content belongs to Medieval History and Topic 13.
16. Pachisi Court: a courtyard designed like a giant board; the record's own added clause about chess is noted as inconsistent with the court's name.
17. Hiran Minar: red sandstone memorial tower for Akbar's favourite elephant Hiran, with elephant-tusk-like projections - a minar as commemorative form, not a mosque tower.
18. After Akbar, no Mughal ruler made any significant architectural contribution at Fatehpur Sikri.
19. The UNESCO citation calls it an "extraordinary ensemble of Mughal architecture" combining Persian-Islamic elements with indigenous Indian styles - the synthesis claim in the heritage record itself.
20. Allahabad Fort: built by Akbar in 1583 near the Yamuna-Ganges confluence; the Allahabad Ashokan pillar is presently inside its premises - relocation and enclosure, not spolia.

### Concept check

**Question:** Fatehpur Sikri is usually memorised as a list of eight or nine buildings. Show how regrouping the same buildings by function turns the list into an argument.

**Model answer:** Sorted by function, the complex resolves into four groups, and each group is a stage for one activity of sovereignty. The religious and commemorative group - the Jama Masjid described as the glory of Fatehpur, the Buland Darwaza commemorating the Gujarat victory, and the white marble tomb of Sheikh Salim Chishti - links legitimacy to conquest and to saintly blessing. The governmental group - the Diwan-i-Aam with its cloisters of one hundred and eleven bays, and the Diwan-i-Khas raised on a square plan with four openings - encodes two different degrees of access in plan. The residential and recreational group - Jodha Bai's Palace with Hindu motifs and blue-glazed tile roofs, the 176-column Panch Mahal, the Pachisi Court, the Hiran Minar - shows domestic and personal life given equally deliberate architecture. The deliberative group is the Ibadat Khana of 1575. The argument that emerges is that the capital was designed so that each function of rule had a purpose-built setting, which no list can show.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Fatehpur Sikri primarily as the site of Akbar's religious experiment. The Ibadat Khana is one building among many, and this topic owns it as a built setting rather than as doctrine. Making the religious policy the organising theme both breaches the subject boundary and loses the architectural argument, which is about functional zoning, material hierarchy and the encoding of access.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**Examine Fatehpur Sikri as evidence that Mughal architecture combined imported and indigenous elements. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

Fatehpur Sikri, founded in 1571 and capital until 1585, carries the evidence within a single imperial complex.

The imported elements are unmistakable: the mosque plan with its qibla orientation, the monumental framed gateway in the Buland Darwaza, Persian inscriptions carrying explicit text, and the four-centred Tudor arch that Akbar introduced.

The indigenous elements sit beside them in imperial fabric. Jodha Bai's Palace blends Hindu motifs with Mughal design and carries blue-glazed tile roofs. The Panch Mahal is a five-storeyed structure of 176 columns - trabeate construction, not arcuate, at the centre of the capital. Local red and buff sandstone is the governing material.

The heritage citation itself records the complex as an extraordinary ensemble combining Persian-Islamic elements with indigenous Indian styles, and the site was inscribed in 1986.

Synthesis here is structural and material, not merely decorative.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = at least three imported elements named precisely 3 + at least three indigenous elements named inside imperial fabric 3 + the Panch Mahal's column count used to make the trabeate point 1 + the UNESCO citation's own synthesis phrase with the inscription year 2 + foundation and capital dates 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 14:** Akbar's capital is red sandstone with one white marble exception. Within a generation the exception becomes the rule, and the change is sharp enough to date buildings by. The next lesson takes the material turn under Jahangir and Nur Jahan, and shows why a single tomb at Agra is the most useful dating instrument in the topic.

## Lesson 14 - Jahangir and Nur Jahan: the marble turn, and material as a dating instrument

Progress: 14/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the reign's recorded architectural modesty, the gardens, Akbar's tomb at Sikandra, Itmad-ud-Daulah's tomb as the first all-marble Mughal work, and the use of material as an internal chronological marker

### Visual: the material line, and what it lets you date

```text
   MATERIAL AS AN INTERNAL CHRONOLOGICAL MARKER
   (independent of inscriptions)

   SULTANATE                          MUGHAL
   ---------                          ------
   reused / local stone  (Slave)
   RED SANDSTONE         (Khilji)
   GREY SANDSTONE        (Tughlaq)
   bare stone            (Lodi)
                                      red sandstone        (Sher Shah:
                                                            Sasaram tomb)
                                      RED SANDSTONE        (AKBAR:
                                       dominant              Agra Fort,
                                                             Fatehpur Sikri,
                                                             Buland Darwaza -
                                                             red AND BUFF)
                                        |
                                        |   ONE EXCEPTION ALREADY:
                                        |   Salim Chishti's tomb -
                                        |   ENTIRELY WHITE MARBLE,
                                        |   i.e. marble = SACRED
                                        v
                                      WHITE MARBLE becomes the CHIEF
                                      BUILDING MATERIAL, "replacing red
                                      sandstone which had been used till
                                      then"          (JAHANGIR / NUR JAHAN)
                                        |
                                      FIRST MUGHAL WORK MADE COMPLETELY
                                      OF WHITE MARBLE:
                                      TOMB OF ITMAD-UD-DAULAH
                                      (father of Noor Jahan), Agra
                                      + "some of the finest PIETRA-DURA
                                        works"
                                        |
                                        v
                                      MARBLE + INLAY AT FULL SCALE
                                                       (SHAH JAHAN: Taj)

   HOW TO USE IT: given an undated Mughal-period structure, its dominant
   material places it before or after the marble turn - a dating technique
   that needs no epigraphy.
   CAUTION: this is a marker of DOMINANCE, not exclusivity. Marble exists
   before (Salim Chishti); sandstone continues after (Red Fort).
```

The diagram is the lesson's argument in a single column. Material is not decoration in this tradition; it is a datable variable, and the sharpest single step in that variable falls in this reign.

### 1. The reign's own record, stated honestly

The standard account is direct: **during the reign of Jahangir, architecture took a backseat as he focused more on paintings and other forms of art.**

That is a genuine finding and should not be softened. It is also the correct route to Topic 07, which owns Mughal painting: the same patron who reduced architectural commissioning expanded painting patronage. An answer that notices this is describing a **shift in the direction of patronage**, not a decline in cultural activity - a distinction that will matter again in Lesson 16.

What is nevertheless recorded as built or overseen:

- **Akbar's tomb at Sikandra**, whose construction Jahangir **oversaw**;
- **a number of gardens designed during his reign, for example the Shalimar Bagh in Kashmir**;
- the **Moti Masjid at Lahore**;
- and, by **Noor Jahan**, **major construction projects like the tomb of Itmad-ud-Daulah, her father**;
- the **tomb of Jahangir**, which was **constructed by his son Shah Jahan at Lahore**.

Note the pattern that has now appeared twice: **Humayun's tomb was commissioned by Bega Begum, Itmad-ud-Daulah's by Nur Jahan, and Jahangir's own tomb was built by his successor.** Mughal funerary architecture on this record is consistently the work of survivors, not of the persons commemorated. That is a patronage observation with real analytical content - it means the tomb is a statement made *about* the dead by the living, which is a different thing from a self-monument.

### 2. The marble turn, stated exactly

The decisive sentence: **it was during this time that white marble became the chief building material, replacing red sandstone which had been used till then. The tomb of Itmad-ud-Daulah was the first Mughal work made completely of white marble. It also has some of the finest pietra-dura works.**

Four components to hold separately:

1. **The change is of chief material** - what predominates, not what exists. Red sandstone does not vanish; white marble takes the leading role.
2. **The threshold monument is named** - the tomb of Itmad-ud-Daulah, the first Mughal work made **completely** of white marble.
3. **The patron is named** - Noor Jahan, commissioning a tomb for her father. This is the second major monument on record commissioned by a woman.
4. **A technique arrives with the material** - **some of the finest pietra-dura works**. As Lesson 5 argued, fine inlay needs a pale, workable ground, so the marble turn and the inlay turn are one event, not two.

### 3. Material as a datable marker: how the technique actually works

The method can be stated explicitly: the material shift, from **Akbar's red sandstone to Jahangir and Nur Jahan's white marble, beginning with Itmad-ud-Daulah's tomb**, functions as an **internal chronological marker independent of inscriptional dating**, and is **reusable as an analytical technique for dating undocumented Mughal-period structures**.

**The procedure, stated as a procedure:**

1. identify the **dominant** material of the structure, not its trim;
2. place it relative to the marble threshold;
3. **test the result against a second variable** - arch profile, dome profile, transition device, ornament system;
4. state the conclusion as a **range with a reason**, never as a date.

**The caution, which is equally examinable.** One standing trap is the assumption that **red sandstone and white marble were used interchangeably throughout Mughal architecture**; collapsing the distinction **loses a useful dating technique**. But the reverse error is just as damaging: treating the marker as exclusive. Two counter-examples settle it. **Sheikh Salim Chishti's tomb at Fatehpur Sikri is entirely white marble and belongs to Akbar's reign**, before the turn. And **Shah Jahan's Red Fort at Delhi** is, by its name and fabric, a sandstone monument after it. The marker tracks **dominance and prestige**, not exclusivity.

**The Sultanate precedent matters here too.** As Lesson 7 showed, the imperial Sultanate phases already run reused stone, red sandstone, grey sandstone and bare stone. So material-as-marker is not a Mughal invention that the Mughals happened to make famous; it is a long-running property of the tradition. Saying so distinguishes an answer that has understood the technique from one that has memorised the red-to-white line.

### 4. The gardens, and the reign's real spatial contribution

The record names **a number of gardens designed during his reign, for example the Shalimar Bagh in Kashmir**, and separately records **Shalimar Bagh in Lahore** under Shah Jahan.

Set against Lesson 4 and Lesson 12, the garden sequence is now continuous: **Bagh-e Babur at Kabul in 1504**, **Charbagh gardens inside Agra Fort**, **Shalimar Bagh in Kashmir under Jahangir**, **Shalimar Bagh in Lahore under Shah Jahan**, and the **Charbagh gardens of the Taj Mahal**. Five named gardens across five generations is enough to argue that the garden is the **most continuous single element in Mughal architecture** - more continuous than material, which changes sharply in this very reign, and more continuous than ornament, which accumulates.

That is a genuinely strong line for a development answer, and it is available only if the gardens are collected rather than mentioned one at a time.

### 5. Akbar's tomb at Sikandra, and the reign's other named works

The standard account records that Jahangir **oversaw the construction of some noteworthy monuments including Akbar's tomb in Sikandra**, and that he **built the Moti Masjid at Lahore**. Beyond the naming and the oversight, no description of either is on record, and none is invented here.

What can be said with discipline: **Akbar's tomb continues the pattern established at Humayun's tomb** - a monumental mausoleum for an emperor, built after his death, under the supervision of his successor. Three generations of Mughal imperial tombs in this record are all posthumous and all supervised or commissioned by others, which is the patronage pattern named in section 1 and is safe to state because it rests on the recorded commissioning facts rather than on any description of the buildings.

### Comparative note: two reigns, two kinds of quietness

| Dimension | Jahangir | Aurangzeb, previewed for Lesson 16 |
|---|---|---|
| Recorded characterisation | Architecture took a backseat; he focused more on paintings and other forms of art | Mughal architecture declined; recorded as puritanical and not actively interested |
| Explanation offered in the record | A shift of patronage towards another art form | A personal-temperament explanation, contested by fuller historical treatment |
| What was nevertheless produced | Gardens, Akbar's tomb at Sikandra, Moti Masjid at Lahore, and - through Nur Jahan - the first all-marble Mughal work | Bibi Ka Maqbara; the continuation of Mughal traditions in regional practice |
| Correct reading | Redirection of patronage, not decline in capability | Reduced imperial commissioning with motive debated and regional continuity intact |

Placing the two side by side now, rather than only in Lesson 16, is deliberate: it establishes that "architecture took a backseat" is a **patronage statement** in both reigns, and that the evidence for continued production sits in the same passages.

### Examination use

This lesson supplies the single most transferable analytical technique in the topic. For a **development demand**, the material line - red sandstone under Akbar, white marble from Itmad-ud-Daulah's tomb - is the organising thread, and it is far stronger than a chronological list of buildings. For a **dating or evidence demand**, state the technique as a procedure and immediately state its limit with the two counter-examples, Salim Chishti's marble tomb before the turn and the Red Fort's sandstone after it. For a **patronage demand**, the two women patrons and the posthumous-tomb pattern are precise, unusual and correct. And the five-garden sequence is available to close any development answer with a continuity claim.

### Revision notes

1. Recorded: during Jahangir's reign architecture took a backseat as he focused more on paintings and other forms of art - a redirection of patronage, and the route to Topic 07.
2. Nevertheless recorded: he oversaw construction of Akbar's tomb at Sikandra; designed a number of gardens, for example Shalimar Bagh in Kashmir; and built the Moti Masjid at Lahore.
3. Nur Jahan undertook major construction projects, including the tomb of Itmad-ud-Daulah, her father.
4. The tomb of Jahangir was constructed by his son Shah Jahan at Lahore.
5. Patronage pattern: Humayun's tomb by Bega Begum, Itmad-ud-Daulah's by Nur Jahan, Akbar's overseen by Jahangir, Jahangir's built by Shah Jahan - Mughal tombs in this record are the work of survivors.
6. The decisive sentence: during this time white marble became the chief building material, replacing red sandstone which had been used till then.
7. The tomb of Itmad-ud-Daulah was the first Mughal work made completely of white marble, and has some of the finest pietra-dura works.
8. Marble and inlay arrive together, because fine inlay requires a pale, workable ground.
9. Material as a datable marker: an internal chronological marker independent of inscriptional dating, reusable for undated Mughal-period structures.
10. Procedure: identify the dominant material, place it relative to the marble threshold, test against a second variable such as arch or dome profile, and state a range with a reason rather than a date.
11. Trap one: assuming red sandstone and white marble were used interchangeably throughout, which destroys the technique.
12. Trap two: treating the marker as exclusive. Salim Chishti's tomb is entirely white marble under Akbar, before the turn; the Red Fort is a sandstone monument under Shah Jahan, after it. The marker tracks dominance and prestige.
13. Sultanate precedent: reused stone, red sandstone, grey sandstone, bare stone - material dating is a long-running property of the tradition, not a Mughal novelty.
14. The five-garden sequence: Bagh-e Babur, Kabul, 1504; Charbagh gardens in Agra Fort; Shalimar Bagh, Kashmir, under Jahangir; Shalimar Bagh, Lahore, under Shah Jahan; the Charbagh gardens of the Taj Mahal. The garden is the most continuous single element in Mughal architecture.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using material as a dating marker, how would you place an undated Mughal-period structure, and what two counter-examples must you state to keep the method honest?

**Model answer:** Identify the dominant fabric rather than the trim. If the building is predominantly red sandstone with marble used for accents, it sits on the earlier side of the threshold, since red sandstone was the principal material through Akbar's reign; if it is predominantly white marble, it sits on the later side, since white marble became the chief building material from the period of Jahangir and Nur Jahan, the first wholly marble Mughal work being the tomb of Itmad-ud-Daulah. The placement must then be tested against a second variable - arch profile, dome profile, transition device or ornament system - and reported as a range with a reason, never as a date. Two counter-examples keep the method honest: the tomb of Sheikh Salim Chishti at Fatehpur Sikri is entirely white marble and belongs to Akbar's reign, before the turn; and Shah Jahan's Red Fort is a sandstone monument after it. The marker therefore tracks dominance and prestige, not exclusivity.

**Misconception to avoid:** Converting a marker of dominance into a rule of exclusivity - assuming that no marble appears before the turn and no sandstone after it. That error is easy to expose with either counter-example and destroys the credibility of the surrounding answer. The companion error, warned against in this topic's advanced framing, is the opposite one: treating the two materials as interchangeable throughout, which throws away a genuinely useful technique.

### Lesson-local Mains practice

**"Mughal architecture can be dated by its material as reliably as by its inscriptions." Discuss. (10 marks, 150 words)**

#### Model answer

The claim holds as a strong tendency and fails as a rule.

Its strength is the sharpness of the recorded shift. Red sandstone was the principal material under Akbar, governing Agra Fort and Fatehpur Sikri, where even the Buland Darwaza is red and buff sandstone. In the period of Jahangir and Nur Jahan, white marble became the chief building material, replacing red sandstone, the threshold monument being the tomb of Itmad-ud-Daulah, the first Mughal work made completely of white marble and carrying some of the finest pietra-dura work. Marble and fine inlay therefore arrive together, giving two correlated variables.

Its limits are equally clear. The entirely white marble tomb of Sheikh Salim Chishti precedes the turn, and Shah Jahan's Red Fort follows it in sandstone.

Material is therefore a reliable indicator of dominance and prestige, and should be corroborated rather than relied on alone.

**Unique scoring note:** **10 marks = the red-to-white shift stated with the threshold monument named 3 + the correlated arrival of pietra dura 2 + both counter-examples supplied 2 + the distinction between dominance and exclusivity 2 + a corroboration-not-substitution verdict 1.**

**Bridge to Lesson 15:** Marble and inlay are now the imperial materials, the garden tomb is an established type, and the double dome has been available for a century. The next reign combines all three at a scale that the record itself calls a climax - and produces the one monument in Indian architecture whose authorship is genuinely contested.
