## Answer spines, side by side

| Demand | Opening move | Evidence spine | Qualification | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Synthesis | State the mechanism, not the label | Technique, craft, material, motif, each with a monument | The documented reuse, named and bounded | A synthesis of practice, not of fixed religious styles |
| Technology | Bending against compression | Voussoir and keystone, mortar, squinch and pendentive, double dome, batter, clamps | Programme travelled with technique | Normalising a system, not inventing it |
| Regional variation | The specification is constant; the solutions are not | Four schools, each as device plus driver plus monument | Minar refusal is political, not climatic | The Bengal roof borrowed back by the Mughals |
| Mughal development | Material as the datable marker | Red sandstone to white marble, with the threshold monument | Salim Chishti's marble; the Red Fort's sandstone | From mass to refinement, with material and programme changing together |
| Sultanate-Mughal comparison | Four axes: scale, material, garden, ornament | Imperial phases against Mughal reigns, with the arcuate-trabeate balance | Trabeate persists throughout | Difference of degree and programme, not of kind |
| Architecture and ideology | Name the physical carrier first | Disproportion, text, access, geometry, repetition | Function is more recoverable than intention | Buildings make claims, and the claim is always carried by something physical |
| Post-Mughal regional courts | Three survivals, three mechanisms | Rajput parallel development; Sikh recomposition; Awadh substitution | Motive for imperial contraction is contested | Commissions contracted; the idiom dispersed |
| Continuity and change | Refuse the clean rupture at 1206 | Corbelled screen, arch-and-lintel entrances, 176 columns, bracket, timber frame | The change in prestige spans and silhouette is real | Superimposition, not replacement |
# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

Compressed recall notes covering every subtopic. Headings are topic-specific; introductory prose from the lessons is not repeated.

## 1. Scope, naming and the four reading questions

- Frame: Arab conquest of Sind 712 AD; by the twelfth century AD the Delhi throne is held by an Islamic ruler - the account's onset of the medieval period. The architectural change dates from the latter, not the former.
- New elements introduced: calligraphy and ornamentation using inlay work. Local architects retained local traditions. Result described as a confluence of Persian with Indian style.
- Names: Indo-Islamic architecture, also Indo-Saracenic architecture. The second name is separately used for the colonial revival style of Topic 05 - two historically distinct usages.
- Four reading questions in order: **load** (engineering), **plan** (liturgy and circulation), **fabric** (economy and craft), **message** (politics and ideology). Message may be argued only after the first three.
- Date-chain control: begun, extended, damaged and repaired, protected, inscribed.
- Owned: structural system, plan types, ornament vocabulary, Sultanate phases, provincial schools, Mughal sequence, Rajput-Sikh-Awadh idioms, and the evidentiary cautions on all of them.
- Not owned: political narrative (Medieval History), religious doctrine (Medieval History and Topic 13), temple form (Topic 03), colonial building (Topic 05), sculpture (Topic 06), painting (Topic 07), heritage policy and counts (Topic 14), craft objects (Topic 12).

## 2. Trabeate and arcuate: the structural distinction

- Trabeate: post and lintel; stone in bending; short spans; multiplied supports. Recorded entries - entrance by lintel; top the shikhara, conical or curvilinear; minars absent.
- Arcuate: arch, vault, dome; stone in compression; long spans; outward thrust requiring abutment.
- True-arch parts: voussoir (wedge-shaped), keystone (central, placed last), springing, intrados and extrados, thrust, abutment, centering.
- Test of a true arch: joints radiate to a centre. Horizontal joints mean corbelling.
- Corbel: successive courses oversail from each side until they meet. The Quwwat-ul-Islam screen is the classic corbelled arcade.
- Generally accepted attribution: first true arch in India at the tomb of Ghiyas-ud-din Balban, Mehrauli, about 1287; earliest surviving true dome at the Alai Darwaza, 1311. A reproduced key in the standard account names the Quwwat-ul-Islam for the arch; both are recorded.
- Mortar: named as the cementing agent; recorded as beginning to be used prominently under the Khiljis, in the same reign as arcuate prominence.
- Minar: introduced by the Islamic rulers around mosques and mausoleums; absent in trabeate practice.
- Trabeate persisted: Tughlaq entrances combined arch and lintel; lintel work continues through the Mughal period. The standard account's summary word "replaced" conflicts with its own Tughlaq passage; combination is followed here.
- What was new after 1206: not the arch or dome as ideas but their scientific, large-scale, mortar-enabled use - plus the new plan types requiring them.
- Reproduced objective item, labelled CSE 2015: of gunpowder, arch-and-dome and the Timurid dynasty, the printed key accepts only the Timurid statement.

## 3. Roofing the square: transition, drum, profile and the double dome

- Transition zone: the band converting a square bay to a circle.
- Squinch: arch, half-cone or corbelled niche across each internal corner; square to octagon to near-circle; faceted signature; the earlier solution.
- Pendentive: concave spherical triangle in each corner; square to circle in one continuous surface; smooth signature; characteristic of mature large-dome work.
- Neither device is named or defined in the standard account; both definitions are ordinary architectural usage supplied here.
- Drum: raises and exposes the dome. Profiles: hemispherical (curve ends at the half-sphere) and bulbous (curve passes it, gathered into a narrow neck). Fluting and finial complete the assembly.
- Double dome: outer shell for the silhouette, inner shell for the ceiling, void between. Recorded as introduced under the Lodis, as "a hollow dome inside the top dome", for two purposes - to give strength, and to lower the inner height.
- Lodi tomb type: only tombs commissioned; hard and bare without lavish decoration; octagonal, about 15 m diameter; sloping verandah; Lodi Gardens and the city of Agra founded by Sikandar Lodi.
- Refinement: mature form at the Lodi-Humayun transition; Sikandar Lodi's tomb of 1517 the early Indian case; Humayun's tomb at monumental scale. No inventor named.
- Significance: separates exterior silhouette from interior volume - the precondition for every later Mughal mausoleum profile.
- Safe temple comparison axis: crowning solution - structural action, interior effect, transition device, silhouette control.

## 4. Mosque, tomb and garden as plan types

- Qibla: the direction of prayer towards Mecca; in India to the west. Qibla wall aligned to it; mihrab the niche marking it.
- Sahn: the courtyard, usually with an ablution tank. Liwan: the covered prayer hall. Iwan: a vaulted space walled on three sides, one side fully open. Pishtaq: the tall rectangular frame enclosing an arched opening.
- Only minar, jaali and charbagh are defined in the standard account; the rest are ordinary architectural terminology supplied here.
- Plan logic: rows form parallel to the qibla wall, so the hall is wide and shallow and wants no internal obstruction - which is why the wide column-free span is worth having.
- Tomb parts: chamber, cenotaph above, burial crypt below, plinth or terrace, chhatri kiosks. Centralised, tending to four-sided symmetry.
- Water: recorded as courtyard pools, fountains and small drains, for three purposes - religious, cooling, decorative.
- Charbagh: a square block divided into four adjacent identical gardens; intersecting channels distribute water; the crossing is the privileged position.
- Named gardens: Bagh-e Babur, Kabul, 1504, one of the earliest surviving Mughal gardens; Charbagh gardens in Agra Fort; Shalimar Bagh, Kashmir, under Jahangir; Shalimar Bagh, Lahore, under Shah Jahan; the Charbagh gardens of the Taj Mahal.
- Four scales of building: structure, complex, fort, city. Cities recorded - Tughlaqabad, Jahanpanah, Ferozabad, the unfinished Dinpanah, Fatehpur Sikri, Shahjahanabad - plus the Sadak-e-Azam with sarais and trees.
- Agra Fort: begun under Akbar, built 1565-1573; interiors mostly Shah Jahan's; Charbagh gardens. Allahabad Fort: 1583, at the Yamuna-Ganges confluence, holding the Allahabad Ashokan pillar.

## 5. The surface and its grammar

- Recorded rule: human and animal figures avoided; calligraphy replaced sculpture as the means of decoration. Qualification: this is general for mosque decoration, not universal for every Indo-Islamic or secular building.
- Arabesque: geometrical vegetal ornamentation; a continuous stem splitting regularly into counterpoised leafy secondary stems, which split again or return to merge with the main stem.
- Geometry used heavily, from decorative pattern to a sense of symmetry.
- Jaali: perforated stone or lattice screen; the buildings had intricate jaali work, signifying the importance of light in Islamic religion. It diffuses light, admits air, permits outward but restricts inward vision, and lightens apparent mass.
- Named jali: the white marble tomb of Sheikh Salim Chishti, with complex geometric patterns, black marble inscriptions and painted floral motifs; the Taj Mahal's lace-like and extremely fine jaali.
- Calligraphic device: the standard account calls it foreshortening; the accurate term is **optical scaling** - letters high on a facade enlarged so the inscription reads evenly from the ground, which is not pictorial foreshortening.
- Pietra dura, also parchin kari: inlay of cut coloured and often semi-precious stones into marble. Finest works at Itmad-ud-Daulah's tomb; listed among the Taj's features. Tied to the marble era and to workshop organisation.
- Elements moving the other way: chhatri (domed kiosk; a defining Sikh feature), bracket (corbelled support enabling the jharokha), batter (sloping walls, introduced by the Tughlaqs, reused in Malwa), bangaldar roof (Bengal's curved hut roof, later borrowed by Mughal architects), and lotus, bell and swastika motifs.
- Roof terminology: bangaldar for the curved form, do-chala for two-sided, chau-chala for four-sided.
- Four ornament systems at four distances: geometry organises, arabesque fills, calligraphy states, inlay refines at close range.

## 6. Early Delhi: adaptation, reuse and evidentiary discipline

- Slave or Ilbari dynasty, 1206-1290; style called Mameluke. Most constructions were remodellings of existing Hindu structures, alongside new monuments.
- Practical reason for early adaptation: immediate need for congregational space, available dressed stone and quarries, and masons trained in trabeate and corbelled technique.
- Qutb Minar, first account: five storeys; initiated by Aibak, who built only the ground floor; next three storeys completed by Iltutmish; fifth storey built by Firoz Shah Tughlaq. Second account: begun by Aibak, substantially completed by Iltutmish, with Firoz Shah Tughlaq later repairing or rebuilding damaged upper work. The discrepancy is recorded, not resolved.
- Transferable method: a single monument can be a layered, multi-generational patronage record.
- Quwwat-ul-Islam: the standard account says converted from a Jain temple; the ASI record of the complex says the mosque inscription records reuse of material from 27 demolished Hindu and Jaina temples. The documented version is used.
- Boundary: evidence of continuity of building fabric across a political and religious transition. Not evidence of a general pattern of destruction, and not reducible to a single conversion.
- Adhai Din Ka Jhonpra, Ajmer: the third named early monument; establishes that early adaptation was not confined to Delhi.
- Spolia explains structure: reused columns and dressed stone carry their own module, which is part of why the earliest Delhi work reads as it does.

## 7. The imperial dynastic sequence

- Delhi Sultanate architecture, 1206-1526 AD, splits into Imperial Style (rulers of Delhi) and Provincial Style (local rulers and fiefs) - a **patronage** classification.
- Imperial members: Slave or Mameluke, Khilji or Seljuk, Tughlaq, Lodi. Provincial members named in the standard account: Bengal, Jaunpur or Sharqi, Malwa or Pathan, Bijapur or Deccan.
- Each ruler imparted his own flavours while the broad stylisation remained the same.
- Khilji, 1290-1320, Seljuk style: red sandstone; arcuate prominence begins; mortar used prominently; Alai Darwaza by Alauddin Khilji; Siri Fort. The three facts are one fact - repeatable arch and dome work.
- Tughlaq, the "crisis period": grey sandstone; strength over decoration; arch and lintel combined; batter introduced - sloping walls for strength; cities of Tughlaqabad, Jahanpanah, Ferozabad.
- Batter mechanism: inward slope lowers the centre of gravity, widens the effective base and resists arch thrust. Later reused by Malwa, credited to the Tughlaqs.
- Lodi: see section 3 for the double dome and tomb type.
- Material read downward - reused stone, red sandstone, grey sandstone, bare stone - shows material dating as a Sultanate practice before the Mughal red-to-white line.

## 8. Bengal: brick, basalt and the exported roof

- Bengal School, 1203-1573 AD; bricks and black basalts; mosques continued the use of sloping Bangla roofs previously used for temples.
- Named examples: Qadam Rasul Mosque and Adina Mosque, placed by the standard account in Malda district of West Bengal; the same section captions a group of monuments in Gaur and Pandua.
- Material logic: no building stone in the delta; excellent brick clay; hard basalt imported for columns, dressings and facings.
- Brick's consequences: many small openings; curved and moulded profiles; moulded brick and terracotta ornament rather than deep relief.
- The bangaldar roof translates the curved bamboo-and-thatch hut roof into masonry; the curve sheds water fast at the ridge and discharges clear of the wall.
- Decisive fact: the Bengal sloping roof was later borrowed by Mughal architects - influence ran upstream.
- One-liner: Bengal roofed its mosques the way it roofed its huts, and the Mughals then roofed their pavilions the way Bengal roofed its mosques.

## 9. Jaunpur and Malwa: screen, refused minar, climate control

- Jaunpur School, 1394-1479 AD, under the Sharqi rulers; also called Sharqi style; Jaunpur a centre of great art and cultural activity.
- Unique feature: bold and forceful characters painted on huge screens in the centre and side bays of the prayer hall. Minars avoided, "as the Pathan style". Named example: Atala Mosque.
- Read the absence as substitution: the screen, not the minar, carries monumental presence. Driver: regional political self-assertion.
- Malwa School, 1405-1569 AD, Dhar and Mandu; also the Pathan school. Most prominent feature: different coloured stones and marbles. Large windows, which the source says **could be** a result of European influence - modality preserved. Stairs used aesthetically; arches and pillars stylised; minars not used.
- Five recorded environmental-adaptation devices: large windows for ventilation; lightly arched pavilions for airiness and coolness; baulis, artificial reservoirs, for water storage in the premises; locally available materials; batter as introduced by the Tughlaqs for strength.
- Named Malwa buildings: Rani Roopmati Pavilion, Jahaz Mahal, Ashrafi Mahal; Hindola Mahal, Mandu.
- Trap defeated: minars are not universal - two whole schools avoided them, falsifying the feature-list definition.
- Honest gap: Jaunpur's building material is not specified on record and is not inferred.

## 10. Bijapur and the Deccan

- Bijapur School, 1490-1656 AD, under Adil Shah; Bijapur or Deccan style. Mosques, tombs and palaces.
- Unique in the use of a three-arched facade and a bulbous dome, the domes almost spherical with a narrow neck. Cornices introduced by Adil Shah.
- Special feature: ceilings treated without any apparent support. Iron clamps and a strong plaster of mortar for strength. Walls richly carved.
- Named example: Gol Gumbaj, the mausoleum of Adil Shah, in Bijapur or Vijayapura. Variant spelling Gol Gumbaz is in common use.
- Bulbous mechanics: the curve passes the half-sphere, so the widest point is above the springing and load concentrates at the neck - which is why tying and strong bedding matter.
- The cornice thread: introduced at Bijapur, curved in Rajput work, shallow in Sikh work.
- Whispering-gallery box: Gol Gumbaj, Victoria Memorial and the Golghar granary, printed as "Bandipore" though the granary is at Bankipore, Patna. The Victoria Memorial is Topic 05's.
- Discipline: "without any apparent support" is what is recorded; no construction geometry is claimed beyond clamps and mortar plaster.

## 11. Gujarat and Kashmir: bounded coverage and a stated gap

- Gujarat is one of the regions this topic requires to be compared but is not among the four provincial schools of the standard account. Kashmir appears there only as a heading and an untexted illustration.
- Gujarat, supplied beyond the four-school account: Jami Masjid, Ahmedabad, 1424, under Sultan Ahmad Shah I - many domes on slender pillars, carved screens, large courtyard; Sidi Saiyyed mosque, Ahmedabad, 1572-73 - pierced lattice windows and the "Tree of Life" jali; Jami Masjid, Champaner, about 1513, under Mahmud Begada - twin minarets, pillared prayer hall, oriel windows, elaborate jali. Inscriptions: Champaner-Pavagadh 2004; Historic City of Ahmadabad 2017.
- Gujarat's analytical value: a sophisticated trabeate stone-carving industry executing an arcuate programme, producing pillared interiors and the finest jali in the tradition.
- Kashmir, supplied beyond the four-school account: Jama Masjid, Srinagar, first built about 1400 under Sultan Sikandar, timber on deodar columns, large square courtyard, high pyramidal roof; Khanqah-e-Moula or Shah Hamadan mosque, late fourteenth century, rebuilt several times after fires, entirely wooden, multi-tiered sloping roof, associated with Mir Sayyid Ali Hamadani. Driver: abundant deodar.
- Kashmir's analytical value: trabeate timber mosques with pyramidal roofs satisfy the same liturgical specification without the arcuate system - the programme is separable from the technology.
- Method: state what the record holds, add only checked supplementation, use it for argument rather than to derive keyed answers, and state the remaining gap.

## 12. The first three Mughal generations

- Babur: mosques at Panipat and Rohilkhand, both recorded as constructed in 1526 AD; reign too short-lived to inspire any new style or technique. Kabuli Bagh Mosque, 1527, Panipat, marking the victory over Ibrahim Lodhi at Panipat in 1526 - the 1526 and 1527 dates are an internal inconsistency, recorded and not resolved. Bagh-e Babur, Kabul, 1504 - the garden precedes the conquest by twenty-two years.
- Humayun: constant power struggle against Sher Shah Suri; could not focus on art and architecture; Dinpanah founded but unfinished; Persian style became prominent. Humayun's Tomb commissioned by Bega Begum in 1558 after his death; UNESCO World Heritage Site 1993; the double dome develops here at monumental scale.
- Sher Shah: few monuments - Qila-i-Kuhna, the Mosque of the Old Fort, Delhi; Rohtas Fort in Pakistan; Sher Shah Suri Masjid, Patna, in Afghan style. His period is the transition from Lodi to Mughal style; his constructions continued Delhi Sultanate traditions. Sadak-e-Azam, the rebuilt Mauryan route later called the Grand Trunk Road, with sarais and trees. Tomb at Sasaram, his birthplace, red sandstone, situated inside a lake.
- What the three generations establish: a garden idea, a court aesthetic, a monumental tomb type, a completed structural device and a continuity - but not yet a distinctive material or ornament system.
- The golden period is dated from Akbar's accession in 1556.

## 13. Akbar: material, arch and a capital built to be read

- Principal material red sandstone; introduced the "Tudor arch", the four-centred arch - wide, low, gently pointed.
- Agra Fort: begun under Akbar, built between 1565 and 1573; most interior structures are Shah Jahan's - Moti Masjid, Diwan-i-aam, Diwan-i-khas; Jahangiri Mahal and Sheesh Mahal, the Turkish Bath, also named; Charbagh gardens.
- Fatehpur Sikri: Agra District, Uttar Pradesh; founded 1571 as the new capital after Agra, following the victorious Gujarat campaign; capital 1571-1585; then Lahore, later back to Agra; UNESCO World Heritage Site 1986; after Akbar no Mughal ruler made any significant architectural contribution there.
- Buland Darwaza: world's highest gateway; commemorates the Gujarat victory; grand entrance to the Jama Masjid; red and buff sandstone; geometric designs and Persian inscriptions, including "The world is but a bridge, pass over it, but build no houses upon it."
- Tomb of Sheikh Salim Chishti: built by Akbar in honour of the Sufi saint whose blessings ensured the birth of Salim, later Jahangir; entirely white marble; exquisite jaali screens with complex geometric patterns, black marble inscriptions and painted floral motifs; cenotaph in the central chamber. In a red sandstone capital, the one marble building is a saint's tomb - marble is sacred before it is imperial.
- Jama Masjid there: "the glory of Fatehpur."
- Jodha Bai's or Mariam-uz-Zamani's Palace: Hindu motifs blended with Mughal design; blue-glazed tile roofs.
- Diwan-i-Aam: extensive courtyard enclosed by cloisters of one hundred and eleven bays; petitions heard and justice dispensed.
- Diwan-i-Khas, the Jewel House: unique in the entire range of Indo-Islamic architecture; square plan; outwardly double-storeyed; internally a single vaulted chamber open floor to roof with an opening on each of four sides.
- Panch Mahal: five storeys, 176 columns - a trabeate building at the centre of the capital.
- Ibadat Khana: constructed 1575; debates with scholars of various religions; forum for interfaith dialogue and for Sulh-i-Kul; led to Din-i-Ilahi in 1582, which never had mass acceptance. This topic owns the built setting only.
- Pachisi Court: a courtyard designed like a giant board; the source's added clause about chess is noted as inconsistent with the court's name.
- Hiran Minar: red sandstone memorial tower for Akbar's favourite elephant Hiran, with elephant-tusk-like projections - a minar as commemorative form.
- Allahabad Fort: 1583, near the Yamuna-Ganges confluence; the Allahabad Ashokan pillar presently inside - relocation and enclosure, not spolia.
- UNESCO citation: an "extraordinary ensemble of Mughal architecture" combining Persian-Islamic elements with indigenous Indian styles.
- Functional grouping of the capital: religious and commemorative, governmental, residential and recreational, deliberative.

## 14. The marble turn under Jahangir and Nur Jahan

- Recorded: architecture took a backseat as Jahangir focused more on paintings and other forms of art - a redirection of patronage, and the route to Topic 07.
- Nevertheless: he oversaw Akbar's tomb at Sikandra; designed gardens, for example Shalimar Bagh in Kashmir; built the Moti Masjid at Lahore. Nur Jahan undertook major projects including the tomb of Itmad-ud-Daulah, her father. Jahangir's own tomb was built by Shah Jahan at Lahore.
- Patronage pattern: Mughal tombs in this record are the work of survivors - Bega Begum, Nur Jahan, Jahangir, Shah Jahan.
- Decisive sentence: white marble became the chief building material, replacing red sandstone which had been used till then. The tomb of Itmad-ud-Daulah was the first Mughal work made completely of white marble, with some of the finest pietra-dura works.
- Marble and inlay arrive together, since fine inlay needs a pale workable ground.
- Material as a datable marker: an internal chronological marker independent of inscriptions, reusable for undated Mughal-period structures.
- Procedure: identify the dominant material; place it relative to the marble threshold; test against a second variable; state a range with a reason, not a date.
- Two traps: assuming the materials were interchangeable throughout, which destroys the technique; and treating the marker as exclusive. Counter-examples: Salim Chishti's marble tomb before the turn; the Red Fort's sandstone after it.

## 15. Shah Jahan: accumulation and authorship

- Under Shah Jahan, Mughal architecture reached its climax.
- Taj Mahal: the finest specimen, exhibiting the full range of grandeur and opulence; built in memory of Arjumand Banu Begum, or Mumtaz Mahal. Listed features: calligraphy, pietra-dura works, the device the source calls foreshortening, Charbagh style gardens, and the use of water in the premises for decoration. Two unique features: jaali work lace-like and extremely fine, and marble carvings in low relief.
- Analytical reading: the monument is designed to reward two viewing distances, and both unique features concern close-range surface behaviour.
- "Climax" is defensible as maximum simultaneous deployment of an accumulated repertoire - not as an aesthetic verdict.
- Other works: Red Fort, Delhi; Jama Masjid, Delhi; Shalimar Bagh, Lahore; the City of Shahjahanabad; and the principal interiors of Agra Fort. The Peacock Throne is recorded as one of the finest examples of metal work of the period; craft objects route to Topic 12.
- The Red Fort is a sandstone monument by the ruler most associated with marble.
- Ustad Ahmad Lahori, 1580-1649: chief Mughal architect under Shah Jahan; associated with the Taj Mahal, Red Fort and Jama Mosque in Delhi; titled Nadir-ul-Asar.
- Correction: named specialists are recorded, but the Taj was a collective achievement, not one mastermind's creation; no single designer is settled fact; do not name him as the uncontested sole architect.
- Structural reason: calligraphic programme, lapidary inlay workshop, quarrying and transport, garden hydraulics, dome and vault construction with centering, and multi-year site organisation are different specialisms.
- Honest gap: the fabric of the Delhi Jama Masjid and of Shahjahanabad is not specified on record.

## 16. Aurangzeb: contraction, contested motive, continuity

- Standard account: Mughal architecture declined; being puritanical, he did not take an active interest in the pursuit of art and architecture. Bibi Ka Maqbara, in memory of Begum Rabia-ud-Daurani, at Aurangabad, described as a poor imitation of the Taj Mahal.
- Fuller historical treatment: fewer major imperial buildings; economy-minded; Mughal traditions continued into regional eighteenth-century practice. Treat motive as debated; separate fewer commissions from survival of the idiom.
- Trap: explaining the reduction solely through a fixed religious personality; patronage, war and fiscal priority also matter.
- Bibi Ka Maqbara control: do not reproduce the aesthetic verdict; compare formally on patronage, scale, material, and garden or mausoleum form. Outcome: a shared centralised garden-tomb type under a reduced resource envelope. The monument's fabric is not specified on record.
- Period-label rule for the whole topic: labels describe imperial patronage volume, and the technical record in the same passage frequently runs against them.
- Continuity confirmed by the source's own sequence: Rajput and Sikh styles in Rajasthan and Punjab, then Awadh of the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries.

## 17. Rajput building

- Rajput constructions were influenced by the Mughal style but unique in size and scope; they generally undertook imposing palaces and forts.
- Feature one: the hanging balcony, jharokha, constructed in all shapes and sizes. Carried on corbelled brackets - a trabeate device in an arcuate period.
- Feature two: cornices built in the shape of an arch such that the shadow took the shape of a bow - ornament made of light, presupposing a strong reliable sun.
- Named example: Hawa Mahal, Jaipur. Driver: court display and climate-screened viewing. Material: local stone.
- Heritage anchors: Hill Forts of Rajasthan inscribed 2013; Jaipur City inscribed 2019.
- The programme is the contribution: palaces and forts are inhabited, accretive, defensively sited and addressed to several audiences at once.
- Two-way exchange evidence: Rajput influenced by Mughal; Sikh heavily influenced by Mughal **and Rajput**; Hindu motifs and blue-glazed tile roofs in the imperial capital; indigenous craftsmen employed, some master architects imported, lotus, bell and swastika reused.
- Formulation: two patronage systems drawing on a shared construction industry, not two styles influencing each other.

## 18. Sikh and Awadh

- Sikh architecture developed in modern-day Punjab; heavily influenced by Mughal and Rajput styles. Features: multiple chhatris or kiosks on the top; shallow cornices and onion domes; fluted domes generally covered with brass and copper guilds for decoration and support; arches decorated by numerous foliations. Named example: Shri Harmandir Sahib or Golden Temple, constructed during the 16th-17th century. Driver: a congregational and devotional programme.
- Every Sikh element is inherited and recomposed to produce a light, vertical, luminous roofline. The metal sheathing is recorded as serving both decoration and support.
- Awadh architecture: mainly around Lucknow; built by the Nawabs of Awadh during the 18th-19th century AD; attempted to preserve Mughal traditions while experimenting with a variety of materials and innovative ideas; both religious and secular structures.
- Material: made mainly using mortar, not marble or sandstone. Recipe recorded as a mixture of brick dust, urad daal, rice husk and tree gum. A shorter summary gives a variant - a mortar-brick-lentil-jaggery mix - which is recorded separately and not merged.
- Driver: cost, speed and famine-relief employment context, reported as a reading of the evidence rather than as a statement of the Awadh passage.
- Bara Imambara, Lucknow: built by Asaf-ud-Daula in 1784; comprises the Asfi mosque, the Bhul-bhulaiya labyrinth and a step well with running water.
- Chota Imambara, Lucknow: built in 1838; a mausoleum for Nawab Muhammad Ali Shah and his mother.
- Rumi Darwaza, the Turkish Gate: built in 1784; between Bara and Chota Imambara; **believed to be** named after the Sufi mystic Jalal ad-Din Mohammad Rumi.
- The mortar decision decouples form from fabric: the Mughal repertoire could be executed without any prestige stone.
- Three survival mechanisms: Rajput parallel development, Sikh recomposition, Awadh substitution.
- Continuities visible here: the step well beside Malwa's baulis; the mausoleum type from the Lodi octagons; the monumental gateway from the Alai Darwaza through the Buland Darwaza to the Rumi Darwaza.

## 19. The five synthesis arguments

- The mechanism: imported structural knowledge, plus indigenous craft labour and trabeate practice, plus local materials, plus borrowed motifs, yields a hybrid building method and regionally differentiated results - one industry absorbing a technology.
- Argument one, material: geology and cost decide fabric; fabric constrains form. Limit: minar refusal, Bijapur's riskier dome, and marble reserved for a saint's tomb are patronage choices no geology compels. Material sets the envelope; patronage chooses within it.
- Argument two, engineering, in eight steps: bending to compression; corbel to true arch; mortar; squinch and pendentive; the double dome; batter; clamps and unsupported-looking ceilings; trabeate persistence.
- Argument three, water: stored (baulis, step well), distributed (channels, drains), displayed (pools, fountains), used as setting (Sasaram in a lake; Allahabad Fort at the confluence). Three recorded purposes - religious, cooling, decorative.
- Argument four, scale: structure, complex, fort, city; the largest architectural act is a capital, and the record includes unfinished and abandoned ones.
- Argument five, message: carried by disproportion, text, plan, access, geometry or repetition. Never asserted without its carrier.
- Circularity reply: some purposes are recorded rather than inferred; inferred ones are disciplined by physical carriers; the reading makes falsifiable predictions. Residual: function is more recoverable than intention.

## 20. Attribution, restoration and present-tense discipline

- Five claim types: who built it, when, what it is made of and how it looked, what it means, what is true of it now.
- Four attribution rules: do not compress reigns into a patron; do not compress workshops into a genius; do not compress a documented instance into a general pattern; do not compress a contested motive into a settled one. All four are the same error - premature compression.
- Restoration: claims about plan, structural system and massing survive repair; claims about surface, carving and finish do not and must be qualified. Repair is evidence of a different kind.
- Anti-essentialism protocol: compare load path, directionality, image policy with its qualification, crowning solution, material and workshop. Do not compare essences. The standard account's contrast of congested Hindu with spacious Indo-Islamic architecture is evaluative framing; the observable difference is wide column-free spans against multiplied trabeate supports.
- Heritage discipline: inscription years for named monuments only; counts and policy route to Topic 14; no construction cost, workforce number or completion date is supported. Inscription years carried: Fatehpur Sikri 1986; Humayun's Tomb 1993; Champaner-Pavagadh 2004; Historic City of Ahmadabad 2017; Hill Forts of Rajasthan 2013; Jaipur City 2019; and Maratha Military Landscapes 2025, safe at year level only.
- Single current linkage: a Hindustan Times report of 2 August 2026 carrying a PTI account of an IIT Roorkee and IIT Kanpur study in the International Journal of Architectural Heritage - a black crust, mainly gypsum formed when sulphur dioxide reacts with calcium-rich dust and moisture, trapping soot, dust and fine metal particles, has spread on the red sandstone of Humayun's Tomb; it weakens the stone rather than merely staining it; it is thickest in rain-sheltered parts. Recommendations: periodic removal by scientifically tested methods, continuous pollution monitoring, evaluation of protective coatings before use, and source-level pollution reduction. Companion study on the Red Fort's sandstone, published in Heritage in 2025 with Ca' Foscari University of Venice and the ASI: crusts of gypsum, bassanite, weddellite, quartz and microcline, 55 to 500 micrometres thick, developing into dendritic crusts causing exfoliation; PM2.5 near the fort above 100 micrograms per cubic metre annually from 2021 to 2023, nearly 2.5 times the national standard, with PM10 often over three times the limit. Read through a dated secondary report; neither primary paper was opened.
- Limits on the current item: it supports a claim about documented chemical damage to the sandstone of two named monuments, not about Indian heritage generally, not a count, and not an assessment of conservation adequacy.

## 21. Answer construction

- Eight demand families: synthesis, technology, regional variation, Mughal development, Sultanate-Mughal comparison, architecture and ideology, post-Mughal regional courts, continuity and change.
- Five qualified theses: synthesis, technology, adaptation, material-chronology, anti-essentialist - each paired with the objection it must survive.
- Ten-mark structure: thesis, trabeate-arcuate as mechanism, two monuments with devices, one qualification, verdict.
- Fifteen-mark structure: thesis, imperial-provincial split as patronage classification, three or four regional devices with monuments, Mughal material chronology with the threshold monument, one regional parallel style, heritage close, verdict.
- Twenty-mark structure: all of the above plus workforce and motif evidence, garden and tomb typology, ideology with carriers, the eighteenth-century continuation, a historiographical caution, and a verdict on the exact wording.
- Fifteen factual-risk controls, from the arch-introduction trap to the restored-surface caution.
- Five verdict lines for synthesis, technology, Mughal development, post-Mughal regional and continuity-and-change demands.
- Evidence rule: device plus named monument; mechanism not label; and identify which of the five dates any date claim is.
- Word budget for 250 words: about 25 thesis, 150 evidence, 40 qualification, 35 verdict. Raise the qualification before the verdict, never after.

   - PDF p. 139, the complete Fatehpur Sikri foundation entry with its location, 1571 foundation after the Gujarat campaign, the 1571-1585 capital period and the move to Lahore and back to Agra; the Buland Darwaza with its superlative, its commemorative purpose, its red and buff sandstone, its geometric designs and Persian inscriptions and the quoted saying; the tomb of Sheikh Salim Chishti with its patron, its dedicatee, its white marble, its jaali screens, black marble inscriptions, painted floral motifs and central cenotaph; and the Jama Masjid described as the glory of Fatehpur;
   - PDF p. 140, Jodha Bai's or Mariam-uz-Zamani's Palace with its Hindu motifs and blue-glazed tile roofs, the Diwan-i-Aam with its one hundred and eleven bays, the Diwan-i-Khas with its square plan and single vaulted chamber, the Panch Mahal with its 176 columns, the complete Ibadat Khana entry of 1575 with Sulh-i-Kul and the 1582 Din-i-Ilahi and its lack of mass acceptance, the Pachisi Court, and the Hiran Minar;
   - PDF p. 141, the statement that no later Mughal ruler contributed significantly at Fatehpur Sikri, the 1986 inscription with the UNESCO description, the Allahabad Fort of 1583 with the Ashokan pillar, and the complete Jahangir entry with the backseat statement, Akbar's tomb at Sikandra, Shalimar Bagh in Kashmir, the Moti Masjid at Lahore, Noor Jahan's projects, the tomb of Itmad-ud-Daulah as the first all-white-marble Mughal work with its pietra dura, the white-marble replacement statement, and Jahangir's own tomb built by Shah Jahan at Lahore;

   - PDF p. 143, the Red Fort, Jama Masjid, Shalimar Bagh at Lahore and Shahjahanabad, the Peacock Throne, and the complete Ustad Ahmad Lahori box with his dates, his role, his associated monuments and the Nadir-ul-Asar title;
   - PDF p. 144, the complete Aurangzeb entry with the decline and puritanism statements and Bibi Ka Maqbara with its dedicatee, its location and the poor-imitation description; the statement that two other styles developed in Rajasthan and Punjab; and the opening of the Rajput entry with its Mughal influence, its uniqueness in size and scope and its palaces and forts;
   - PDF p. 145, the jharokha and the bow-shadow cornice with Hawa Mahal, and the complete Sikh entry with its Mughal and Rajput influence, multiple chhatris, shallow cornices and onion domes, fluted domes with brass and copper guilds, foliated arches and Shri Harmandir Sahib of the 16th-17th century;
   - PDF p. 146, the complete Awadh architecture entry with its Lucknow focus, its 18th-19th century dates, its preservation of Mughal traditions with experimentation, its religious and secular structures, its mortar material statement with the four-ingredient recipe, and the Bara Imambara of 1784 by Asaf-ud-Daula with the Asfi mosque, the Bhul-bhulaiya and the step well;

## Bounded material-fact pass

10. **One finite pass, pre-declared and then closed.** The bounded-research rule permits a single material-fact pass for genuinely disputed points and for a short pre-declared list of standard connecting names, terms and dates whose omission would create a real syllabus gap. The pass covered exactly eight pre-declared items and was then closed.
    - **Item one, a disputed attribution.** The core art source reproduces an objective item asking where the first extant true arch is found, and its own key selects the Quwwat-ul-Islam mosque. A single reference check established the standard attribution of the first true arch in India to the **tomb of Ghiyas-ud-din Balban at Mehrauli, Delhi, of about 1287**, with the **Alai Darwaza of 1311** carrying the **earliest surviving true dome**, and with the Quwwat-ul-Islam's screen arches understood as corbelled rather than radial. Both positions are recorded in Lesson 2 and in the PYQ section; neither is silently dropped, and no answer letter is asserted for the reproduced item.
    - **Item two, undefined structural terminology.** Neither the core art source nor the canonical owners define **squinch** or **pendentive**, though the canonical Basic owner lists both in its terminology-control clause. The pass established their structural definitions and the general association of squinches with early domed chambers and of pendentive-based transitions with mature large-dome work. Carried as reference-level findings in Lesson 3 and not attached to a specific monument list.
    - **Item three, undefined mosque-plan terminology.** **Qibla, qibla wall, mihrab, sahn, liwan, iwan** and **pishtaq** are not defined in the permitted evidence base, although the topic cannot be taught without them. The pass established standard architectural definitions for each. Carried as reference-level findings in Lesson 4.
    - **Item four, a date for the earliest Indian double dome.** The canonical owners identify Sikandar Lodi's tomb as an early Indian case without a date. The pass established **1517** for that tomb at Delhi and confirmed it as the earliest surviving Indian example. Carried in Lessons 3, 7 and 12.
    - **Item five, roof terminology.** The core source names only the "Bangla roof". The pass confirmed the family terms **bangaldar** for the curved form, **do-chala** for a two-sided and **chau-chala** for a four-sided sloping roof, and confirmed that Mughal adoption of these Bengal roof forms became significant in later north Indian building. Carried in Lessons 5 and 8.
    - **Item six, Gujarat.** Gujarat is named in the canonical Basic owner's geography-control clause among the regions to be compared but is absent from the core source's four provincial schools. The pass established the **Jami Masjid at Ahmedabad, 1424, under Sultan Ahmad Shah I**; the **Sidi Saiyyed mosque at Ahmedabad, 1572-73, with its "Tree of Life" jali**; the **Jami Masjid at Champaner, about 1513, under Mahmud Begada**; and the inscription years **2004** for Champaner-Pavagadh Archaeological Park and **2017** for the Historic City of Ahmadabad. Carried as reference-level findings in Lesson 11 and used for argument only.
    - **Item seven, Kashmir.** The core source carries only a heading and an untexted illustration captioned "Architecture in Kashmir". The pass established the **Jama Masjid at Srinagar, first built about 1400 under Sultan Sikandar**, timber on deodar columns with a high pyramidal roof, and the **Khanqah-e-Moula or Shah Hamadan mosque**, of the late fourteenth century, rebuilt several times after fires, entirely wooden with a multi-tiered sloping roof and associated with Mir Sayyid Ali Hamadani, with abundant deodar as the material driver. Carried as reference-level findings in Lesson 11; no school, dates or feature list is constructed for the region.
    - **Item eight, heritage inscription years for named monuments.** The canonical owners permit inscription years on named monuments and supply only Fatehpur Sikri 1986, Humayun's Tomb 1993 and the Maratha Military Landscapes 2025. The pass established **2013** for the Hill Forts of Rajasthan and **2019** for Jaipur City, which are needed for Lesson 17's regional close, alongside the Gujarat years in item six. Counts, policy and status changes are routed to Topic 14 throughout, and no cost, workforce or completion figure is attached to any monument anywhere in this session.
11. No other external research was conducted. No optional source, exhaustive corroboration or decorative current affairs was pursued.

## Current-affairs source used

12. **Lesson 20, the single current linkage.** Search run: "Indo-Islamic monument conservation and material decay, Archaeological Survey of India, 2026". Item used: a **Hindustan Times** report carrying a **Press Trust of India** account of a study conducted jointly by the **Indian Institutes of Technology at Roorkee and Kanpur** and published in the **International Journal of Architectural Heritage**, concerning black crust on the red sandstone of **Humayun's Tomb**. The report's own timestamp places it at **2 August 2026**, and its text attributes the PTI account to that Sunday. Recorded content used: that a black crust has spread on the tomb's red sandstone and is attributed to Delhi's polluted air; that the crust is mainly **gypsum**, formed when **sulphur dioxide** reacts with **calcium-rich dust and moisture**; that the gypsum layer then traps **soot, dust and fine metal particles** from vehicle emissions, construction, road dust, biomass burning and industrial pollution; that the damage is **not merely cosmetic** because the reactions gradually **weaken the sandstone**; that the **thickest crust developed in rain-sheltered parts** while exposed surfaces carried thinner deposits; and that the authors recommend **periodic removal using scientifically tested conservation methods**, **continuous pollution monitoring**, **evaluation of protective coatings before use** and **source-level pollution reduction**. The same report records an earlier companion study by essentially the same team on the **Red Fort's sandstone**, published in the journal **Heritage** in **2025** with **Ca' Foscari University of Venice** and the **Archaeological Survey of India**, finding crusts of **gypsum, bassanite, weddellite, quartz and microcline**, ranging from **55 to 500 micrometres** thick and developing into dendritic crusts causing **exfoliation**, with annual average **PM2.5 near the fort above 100 micrograms per cubic metre between 2021 and 2023**, nearly **2.5 times** the national standard, and **PM10 often more than three times** the limit. The item was read directly from the newspaper's own page; neither the primary journal article nor the earlier *Heritage* paper was opened in this pass, and the item is therefore attributed to that dated secondary account.
13. No other current item is used anywhere in this session. Twenty of the twenty-one lessons record truthfully in their pre-teach checklists that no current linkage is required.

## Evidence, discrepancy and prohibition controls

- **Disclosed PYQ routing position one.** The Civil Services 2019 Kalyana Mandapas item is routed by the local Prelims ledger to this topic, while this topic's canonical Basic owner records a substantive-ownership correction on the ground that a Kalyana Mandapa is a Hindu temple hall type owned by Topic 03. Both positions are stated; the ledger is not overwritten; and this session claims only the bounded contribution of Islamicate vocabulary for Vijayanagara's secular and royal buildings.
- **Disclosed PYQ routing position two.** The 2023 GS-I Question 12 on Sultanate technological change is routed by the ledger to Medieval Indian History. That routing is accepted; this topic is recorded as a support owner for the architectural half only; and because no official 2023 paper is held locally, the question's wording is reproduced only as the ledger's neutral rendering and is explicitly flagged as not verbatim.
- **Disclosed source discrepancy one - the Qutb Minar's upper work.** The core art source records that the fifth storey was built by Firoz Shah Tughlaq; the canonical owners record that he repaired or rebuilt damaged upper work. Both are stated in Lesson 6 and in the register notes, and the neutral phrase "later upper-storey work under Firoz Shah Tughlaq" is used where a single formulation is required.
- **Disclosed source discrepancy two - the Quwwat-ul-Islam reuse record.** The core art source describes the mosque as converted from a Jain temple; the canonical owners, citing an ASI record, describe its inscription as recording reuse of material from twenty-seven demolished Hindu and Jaina temples. The documented version is used throughout, the alternative formulation is recorded, and the difference between a conversion claim and a material-reuse claim is taught explicitly in Lesson 6.
- **Disclosed source discrepancy three - arcuate replacing trabeate.** The core art source's summary states that the arcuate style replaced the traditional trabeate style, while its own Tughlaq passage records arch and lintel combined in entrance design. This session follows the combined-use evidence, which the canonical owners also carry, and states the conflict in Lesson 2.
- **Disclosed source clipping - the trabeate-arcuate comparison table.** The printed table at PDF p. 128 is cut off in its arcuate column; only the trabeate entries and the column headings survive legibly, as confirmed by rendering the page at high resolution. The arcuate entries used in this session are taken from the canonical Basic owner's reconstruction of the same table and are labelled as such in Lesson 2.
- **Disclosed source inconsistency one - Babur's mosque dates.** The core art source states that the Panipat and Rohilkhand mosques were both constructed in 1526 AD and, in the same passage, dates the Kabuli Bagh Mosque at Panipat to 1527. Both are recorded in Lesson 12 and neither is silently selected.
- **Disclosed source inconsistency two - the Pachisi Court.** The core art source describes the court as designed like a giant board and, in the same sentence, states that Akbar is believed to have played chess there, although the court is named for pachisi. The description is reported as printed and the inconsistency is noted in Lesson 13.
- **Disclosed source error - the Golghar granary's location.** The whispering-gallery box at PDF p. 135 prints "Golghar Granary in Bandipore"; the granary is at Bankipore in Patna. Both the printed form and the correction are recorded in Lesson 10, and neither is used as an examinable fact beyond the whispering-gallery list itself. The Victoria Memorial in the same list is routed to Topic 05.
- **Disclosed source variance - the Awadh mortar recipe.** The core art source gives the mortar as a mixture of brick dust, urad daal, rice husk and tree gum; the canonical Advanced owner's comparative framework gives a mortar-brick-lentil-jaggery mix. The two agree on mortar, brick and lentil and differ on the remainder. Both are recorded in Lesson 18; they are not merged into a composite list.
- **Disclosed evidence gap one - Kashmir in the core source.** The core art source carries a heading and an illustration captioned "Architecture in Kashmir" at PDF p. 147 with no accompanying text, confirmed by block-level extraction of that page. Kashmir is therefore covered only through the declared bounded reference findings of Lesson 11, with no school, dates or feature list asserted.
- **Disclosed evidence gap two - Jaunpur's material.** Neither the core source nor the canonical owners specify the building material of the Jaunpur or Sharqi school. No material is inferred, and the gap is stated in Lesson 9 and in the comparison table.
- **Disclosed evidence gap three - Bibi Ka Maqbara.** The permitted evidence base supplies no fabric, dimensions or construction date for the monument. The formal comparison with the Taj is therefore run only on the axes that can be evidenced, and the incomplete axis is declared in Lesson 16.
- **Disclosed evidence gap four - the Delhi Jama Masjid and Shahjahanabad.** The permitted evidence base does not specify the fabric of either. None is inferred, and the gap is stated in Lesson 15.
- **Disclosed evaluative framing, not adopted as a finding.** The core art source states that Hindu architecture had become too congested while Indo-Islamic architecture introduced spaciousness, massiveness and breadth. This session records the statement, treats it as the source's evaluative characterisation rather than as a measured property, and teaches the observable structural difference - wide column-free spans against multiplied trabeate supports - in its place, in Lesson 20.
- **Disclosed inference boundary - the Awadh driver.** The famine-relief employment context given by the canonical Basic owner as a driver for Awadh's mortar construction is not stated in the core source's Awadh passage. It is reported in Lesson 18 as the canonical owner's reading rather than as a recorded fact of the passage, and the socio-economic history is routed to Modern and Medieval Indian History.
- **Disclosed naming variants, recorded and not normalised.** Gol Gumbaj as printed in the core source against the common form Gol Gumbaz; Bijapur against Vijayapura, both recorded as current; Jaali against jali; Noor Jahan as printed against the common form Nur Jahan; Jodha Bai's Palace against Mariam-uz-Zamani's Palace, both given by the source; Bangla roof as printed against the bangaldar family terms established in the bounded pass; Adhai Din Ka Jhonpra as printed; Ibrahim Lodhi as printed against Lodi elsewhere in the same source; and Huchimalligudi-style orthographic variation is not at issue in this topic.
- **Prohibited sources.** No artifact under the permanently excluded final learner-package tree was read, searched, cited, compared against or used to derive any finding at any stage of this session, in accordance with the permanent live-session source exclusion. No learner-v2 artifact was used. The topic-level complete package at `upsc-ai-kit\knowledge\Indian-Art-and-Culture\04_Indo-Islamic-and-Regional-Architecture_Complete-Topic-Package.md` was not used as a substantive source, because the bounded source order governing this session restricts substantive sourcing to the complete Basic owner, the complete Advanced owner, one core OCR art source, verified PYQs through 2026, one bounded material-fact pass and one current linkage.
- **No fabricated evidence.** No date, dimension, cost, workforce figure, inscription, quotation, monument attribution or answer letter has been invented, reconstructed from memory or silently completed anywhere in this session. Where the evidence base stops, the stop is recorded.
